The current stage's `cue` of every other track they own (secret ladders, quests, puzzles, and agendas that aren't public), while the track still keeps something from the POV: a stage truth not yet revealed, or the track itself, which the POV may not be told of (public, revealed, or `known_by` and known). And the entity's own `cues` (`EntityDef.cues`), each keyed to exactly one secret: a lore entry, while it is secret and its gate doesn't hold; a track, while it keeps something from the POV as above; or a story flag, until it is set. A cue keyed to none, to several or to one the world doesn't have never shows. Cues are read live from the world, so an Upgrade changes them in a running chat.
- **Hint risk:** a cue, an agenda's words or a decision's label that carries an unrevealed secret (`findLeaks` against `unrevealedSecrets`) or names anything the POV can't see (`namesHidden`: a hidden person by any word of their name, a hidden thing, place, track label or secret lore title by all its words) is dropped on its own, and the rest of the person's lines stay.
- **Narrator note:** the shipped template opens with a note: the narrator knows only what the POV knows, these lines say how people behave, and it never states what a cue only hints at. It is the section's template, so a world edits it like any other.
- **Not a fact:** the helpers' fact lines (`factLines`, read by `brief`, `outline`, `choices`, `contradiction` and the step placeholders) leave the section out. Its cues are hints to be played, and a prose helper given one could state it; the contradiction check would read a reply that plays a cue loosely as contradicting it.
- **Lint** (`castlife.cues`): an entity's cue with no text, standing for no secret, for more than one or for one the world doesn't have, or for one that hides nothing (lore that isn't secret, a public track with no truth), and cues on anything but a person; an entity's cue that holds a track's truth or a phrase of secret lore (an error), or names an entity, place, track or secret lore entry the player may not know (a warning: it is dropped while that stays hidden). A stage cue of a person's own track gets the name warning too, and names are read as the runtime reads them (`namesAny`), so a part of one counts. A stage cue can also show in the Quest panel and section, so one that says a secret entity's or place's name any other way than whole ("Vex" for Vex Morrow) is an error; truths and whole secret names there are `story.leaky_label`'s.

### 6.7 Hidden JSON blocks
- **Why:** a preset may ask every reply to end with a `<json>…</json>` block (a tracker, a ledger). In a world chat the names in it would trigger World Info entries and mislead the engine's own reads, and the player sees raw JSON.
- **The scripts:** four regex scripts the engine creates and owns (`backend/json-hiding.ts`, in the regex panel's LumiRPEngine folder). World Info's keyword and vector scans read a chat's messages as saved, before any prompt script runs (assembly activates World Info on the stored messages and applies prompt scripts to the assembled prompt afterwards; the dry run and the council read stored messages too), so the exclusion has to be saved with the reply, and everything else should read the reply without it. The frontend runs display scripts by sort order whatever their scope; the backend runs response and prompt scripts global first, then the chat's, each by sort order. All four match a block as the host reads one, `<json>` in any case and without attributes up to the first `</json>` outside a JSON string, so a wrapper never lands inside a block, and the block stays byte for byte as written, so `{{jsonBlock}}` and other readers still find it.
  - **LumiRPEngine · JSON blocks out of World Info**, the world chat's, runs on `response` (a Continue bakes only what it added) and again on an edit, last among the chat's scripts (sort order 100,000), after the global and character ones: it wraps each block in AI output as `<span wi-exclude><json>…</json></span>`, which the scans skip and which hides nothing. A lookbehind keeps a block from being wrapped twice, or inside an earlier build's wrapper.
  - **LumiRPEngine · JSON blocks as written (display)** and **(prompt)** are the user's: global, at sort order −100,000, so each runs before every other script on its target, and they take that span off a block, matching nothing else. A preset's display script that draws the block as a tracker, a prompt script that strips old blocks from the history (anchored on the block or not), markdown and the narrator all read the reply as the AI wrote it.
  - **LumiRPEngine · Hide JSON blocks**, the world chat's, runs on `display` only, at sort order 100,000, after the other display scripts, and wraps each block still raw in `<span hidden>`: a block a preset drew as a tracker isn't raw any more, and keeps its tracker.
- **Lifecycle:** a world chat's two scripts are created when a world is attached, and checked the first time the engine meets a world chat after it starts (the chat opened in the UI, or its generation), so a chat attached before this build, or one whose scripts were deleted in the regex panel, gets them back; the user's two unwraps are checked with the first world chat the engine meets in a run, before the chat's own. The single script an earlier build wrote (on both targets, wrapping in `<div hidden wi-exclude>`) is rewritten in place as the hiding, a script an earlier build wrote differently or sorted elsewhere is brought up to date, and extra copies are removed; whether a script is switched on stays the user's. **Settings → Preset → Hide JSON blocks in world chats** (on by default) removes every world chat's hiding when turned off, and when turned on brings back the open chat's at once and every other one's as the engine meets it; the exclusion and the unwraps stay, so replies show their blocks as the AI wrote them and World Info still skips them. Deleting a world removes its chats' scripts, since they aren't world chats any more (each script records its world), and they never come back: the engine writes a chat's scripts only while its world exists. The host doesn't delete a chat-scoped script with its chat, so when it reports a chat deleted (`CHAT_DELETED`, sent as `{ id }`) the engine removes that chat's scripts; a chat deleted while the engine wasn't running keeps them until its world is deleted. The unwraps stay through all of this, since the replies saved meanwhile keep the span. Chats that aren't world chats never get scripts of their own.
- **The engine's own reads** of a reply (`readableReply`), while hiding is on, leave out every block, with its wrapper, whether the display hides it or a preset draws it as a tracker: `extract`, the lore prefetch and what the player has seen said, `contradiction`, `choices` and `effects` templates (`{{reply}}`), the check for a Buy told again, and Save to lore of a whole reply. A Continue's read is matched against the whole message, so a block it split is left out whole. A reply an earlier build saved with `<div hidden wi-exclude>` around a block stays hidden in the display whatever the setting, so the block inside one is always left out, whitespace inside the wrapper or not; with hiding off, every other block is read as saved, span and all. The ledger keeps the reply as saved.

### 6.8 The preset's say
What the preset a chat generates with says applies to that chat (`backend/preset-binding.ts`; for authors, `docs/PRESETS.md`). The preset is the host's own pick for the chat (`presets.getActive`, as prompt assembly makes it: the selected preset, else the one the chat's connection links), with the block states its profile bindings set for the chat, its character, persona or connection; a block they switch off places nothing. When the host can't say, the engine can't tell where the sections go: the engine run stops, and the chat's flag "The engine didn't finish, so nothing was generated" gives the host's reason. What a page reports as active isn't read, and nothing stands in for the host's answer. The blocks are read through the presets API once per preset revision (`cache_revision`) and block states, in the engine run for its phases and its sections, and again by the reply's guard; Settings → Preset reads the selected preset. Only that preset counts: another preset saved (Workshop, another extension) changes nothing, and a save of this one makes the engine run again only when it changes what the turn's sections use (§5.1).
- **Placement:** each section has an outlet twin in the world's book (outlet-only, `outlet_name` `lre_<slot>`), and the narrator rules have one at `lre_narrator`. When an enabled block that assembles for every generation (no injection trigger) holds `{{outlet::lre_<slot>}}`, the engine run fills the twin instead of the section's own entry, so the section lands at the preset's outlet; otherwise the world's placement applies (§4.2, §6.4). Precedence: the preset's outlet, then the section's Position, then where the host's editor put a native entry.
- **Twins are a turn's too:** a twin holds a section only for the turn that filled it, like the section's own entry (§6.4): the reply's guard fills it, it is emptied once that reply's prompt is assembled, and a generation with no turn of its own finds it empty. What the preset places is read in the engine run and checked again by the reply's guard: when that preset's `lre_` outlets, guidance or narrator rules changed in between, the engine runs again (§5.1).
- **A chat behind the head:** a chat on an older revision places each section where its own revision says: the engine run places the book's entries for that revision while the chat holds the book (§6.4).
- **Outlets the host won't assemble:** a block limited to some generation types or to characters with certain tags (`characterTagTrigger`), and any switched-on block after the first under a radio category, place nothing. A block the host leaves out for reasons the engine can't see (prompt-activation regex scripts) still counts as placing its outlets: the twin activates, so the section reads as delivered, though no assembled block holds it. The interceptor-era test build that caught this went with the interceptor; a dry-run check of the routed outlets is a follow-up.
- **Guidance:** a switched-off block named "RPEngine · <step>" (`interpret`, `extract`, `brief`, `outline`, `choices`, `npc.choose`, `judge`, `contradiction`, `puzzle.answer`) is appended to that helper's prompt, after the world's step template or the built-in prompt and its output contract, as style and rules that never change the contract; on the Decision route it rides in the decision's state as `rules`. The engine resolves no macro in it: its braces, control characters and shorthands are neutralized (§6.3). Lines starting with `//` are notes and are skipped, and the rest is cut at a word to 1,500 characters. A switched-on block of that name reaches the narrator in every chat, so the engine doesn't read it. The first block of a name counts.
- **Narrator rules:** a switched-off "RPEngine · narrator" block reaches the narrator in world chats only, as `[Narrator rules]` in the narrator entry (before the character) or its twin at `{{outlet::lre_narrator}}`, apart from the section budget.
- **Some outlets but not all:** a preset that places some of the world's sections with their outlets and leaves others out lays the prompt out itself, so the rest landing at the world's placement, outside its blocks, is likely a mistake (one placed everything but People). The preset lint (D32) warns about it (`preset_partial_outlets`) with the outlets missing, read from the preset's own blocks as above, since the dry run resolves outlets away.
- **Settings → Preset** shows the active preset, the `lre_` outlets its enabled blocks hold with the section each places, and each guidance block's state, and **Add RPEngine blocks to this preset** adds the missing ones, switched off and holding only `//` notes, under an "RPEngine" category (`lre.preset`). They are spliced into the preset's stored blocks, which are written back once, each exactly as stored, at the revision they were read at: the host's block API would re-normalize every block of the preset on each insert and drop what it doesn't know, a sealed block's protection among it.

### 6.9 Diagnostics and the activity line
What the engine did for a chat, step by step, and what it is doing now (`backend/diagnostics.ts`, `shared/diagnostics.ts`).
- **Records:** each turn (keyed by its generation id; an engine run's records start under its own id and follow the generation that takes its turn) records its phases as steps: `pre` (lore, `interpret`, the judge, `npc.choose`, minds and a time skip's beats), `plan`, `prose` (brief and outline), writing the sections (`sections`), `read` and `memory`, then the turn itself. Each step has its start, end and duration and its outcome: `ok`, `skipped` with why, or `failed` with why and, where the engine can tell, a fix ("raise Max tokens for outline in Settings → Steps and connections"). Each model call made inside a step joins it with its purpose, connection, model, tokens in and out, `max_tokens`, `finish_reason`, time and error, and, when it was made again after a transient failure, which attempt it was. A step whose last call failed, came back empty or was cut off (`finish_reason` length) is failed even when it fell back quietly, and in the engine run any failure stops it (§5.1). Also recorded: a generation the guard couldn't stop, a reply's generation that failed or was stopped, a prepared turn dropped because the chat changed, every error a ledger operation or a request couldn't recover from, the sections written (the generation path, each section, the preset outlet it goes to and whether it went out with text; failed when no section had text), and any request or state view slower than a second.
- **Storage:** the last 400 records per chat, in `userStorage/runs/<chat>/diagnostics.json`, read the first time they are needed and written debounced, so a restart keeps them. Each record is also a line in the user's `diagnostics.log`, with its level (`DEBUG`, `INFO`, `WARN`, `ERROR`) and its turn's short id.
- **Secrecy:** records are GM-level (a reason may name someone the player hasn't met), so like the GM view they answer only `lre.diag`, never a push; Settings → Diagnostics asks for them only once the player opens its curtain.
- **Activity line:** while a turn runs the backend pushes `lre.activity` with a label and the turn's start: "Running the engine…", "Reading your action…", "Asking 3 minds…" (the model calls under way, minds counted), "Filling the prompt…", "Waiting for another chat of this world…", "Ready; reopen the chat to continue" (a restart no page has started yet), "Already started in another window" (to a page that doesn't hold the restart), "Ready: press Send for the reply", "Waiting for the reply…", "Reading the reply…", "Updating memory…". It carries a step's label and nothing else, so it is player-safe. A turn with a failed step ends on a failure ("Failed: Planning") that stays until the next turn or Dismiss. The HUD pill, the phone's status line and the drawer show it with the time taken; the failure links to Settings → Diagnostics. While an engine run or a reply read is under way the activity says it can be cancelled (`cancellable`), and the drawer's running line has **Cancel**, which stops it (§5.1); waiting for the reply or for Send, there is nothing to cancel and no Cancel.

### 6.10 Usual routines for the narrator
Routines are informative by default (§8): the engine never moves Live, Near or Far characters by them, so for those the narrator's soft line is how their routine reaches the story, and the narrator decides what they do. While the world's `routineSettings.narrator` is on (the default), People carries a hint line (`usualRoutineHints`, in `packs/castlife/core/usual.ts`) for each person at the POV's place that the POV can see and who acts as it knows, whose routine role (§8) isn't `off`: "Maya usually: weekdays, working the counter at Daybreak Café 07:00–15:00; on Tue, swimming at the gym in the evening." A lead line says these are background the narrator may use or ignore, never a fact to state, and that what the story establishes comes first.
- **What it tells:** their usual routine around now, at most three clauses: the blocks of their routine in force (the basic needs aside) and the spans of their own place and activity schedules they still follow in this chat (a place and an activity on the same days at the same times read as one clause), today's under way or still to come first. It reads the routine as the world and the chat set it, never the day the planner lays, so nothing the planner filled free time with (meals, friends, invitations) is told as a habit.
- **The player's view:** never a private block, a place the POV can't see, an activity schedule of someone whose activity it may not see, or a clause carrying an unrevealed secret or a hidden name (`findLeaks`, `namesHidden`), each dropped on its own.
- **Why People:** People names everyone with the POV that it can see, the people the Minds section tells of included; Stance leaves those out (§24.3), and with minds on they are the Live cast the line matters most for. As a hint it never displaces a section and never sends People on its own.
- **Where a move goes:** the reply's read gets the same lines with ids (§9.4), never as facts, so a move the narrator writes toward someone's usual place ("off to their usual lunch spots") lands there by the location mode, like a move to a named place.

### 6.11 The engine feed
What goes into each section, and whose knowledge it comes from (`engine/feed.ts`; decision 1 of the October review).
- **Settings:** a section (`SectionTemplate`) may carry `enabled` (false leaves it out of the prompt), `maxLines` (its first lines; its hints go in whole only within it), `maxTokens` (lines past it cut from the end; a section none of whose lines fit is clipped), `include` (the renderer sources that fill it; absent or empty, every renderer of its own slot) and `scope`. Counts are whole and at least 1. A section with none of them delivers as before. The world sets them in Build → Sections; a malformed one is a lint error (`section_feed`) and reads as none, an `include` source no pack renders is a warning (`section_source`), and a section nothing fills, by its slot or its `include`, is `section_unfed`.
- **Per chat:** the GM sets a section's settings in a chat with the `feed` GM edit op (GM → This chat → Sections), an `engine.feed` event in the engine's state slice: the chat's settings stand in the world's place for that section, whole (an empty one is the plain section, so the GM can lift a cap or a source list the world set), `to: null` returns the section to the world's, and the edit's Undo takes it away. A chat that never had one holds no slice key, so older chats fold as before. The event has no player line.
- **Sources:** a renderer's `source` names it for `include` (absent, its slot): `location`, `calendar` and `weather` (Scene), `people`, `routines` and `present_gear` (People), `stance` and `cues` (Stance), `conditions` (Outcome), `money`, `gear`, `vehicles`, `quest`, `place`, `property` and `minds`. The turn's own lines (the Outcome's results, the Montage, the Outline, Scene's elapsed time) stay with their own section. Place lore joins the Place section from a renderer the lore owner registers on the `place` slot with source `place_lore`, its lines carrying their World Info entries (below).
- **Scopes:** `player` (the default) is what the POV knows, as every section has always read; `scene` adds what the people with it know or can see by the visibility rules (`canSeeEntity(knower, id)`, `canSeePlace(knower, place)`, `known_by` through what they know), so never a `secret` thing, place, agenda or track, nor a track's truth: those are the GM's alone; `gm` adds everything. A renderer gives a scope's lines through `feed(ctx, scope)` as `FeedLine`s, each `known` by the POV (a plain line), by people in the scene (`who` names them, as the POV may know them) or by no one there (`gm`); one without `feed` gives its player lines in every scope. A fact beyond the POV's is marked for the narrator ("[unknown to you; known to Maya]", "[unknown to you; GM only]"), and the narrator rules then lead with the engine's rule: marked facts are for steering, never to be revealed, narrated as known or acted on by the player character. Each section's scopes:
  - **People:** in `scene`, someone here the POV can't see whom a person it can see knows; in `gm`, anyone here. Its `present_gear` source tells what each person with the POV that it can see visibly wears and carries: worn and equipped things and what they hold, never what is in a bag or a pocket, at most 6 each; in `scene`, a thing on them the POV can't see that they can, as theirs to know; in `gm`, any other thing on them too.
  - **Scene:** in `scene` and `gm`, the place around the POV's and a nearby exit it can't see, as known to whoever with it can (else the GM's).
  - **Stance:** in `scene`, each person's `known_by` agendas, at their stage, as theirs to know; a secret agenda only in `gm`.
  - **Money:** in `scene`, the wealth of each person with the POV, as theirs to know; in `gm`, of anyone there.
  - **Gear:** the POV's own things only as it can see them; in `gm`, a thing on it it doesn't know of.
  - **Vehicles:** above (§6.3).
  - **Quest:** in `scene`, a `known_by` quest the POV doesn't know that someone with it knows, at its stage; in `gm`, every track it doesn't know, a secret one included, with its truth.
  - **Place** (`place`, the Space pack, rank 11): the place's `description` when the POV can see the place (standing in one it can't, the description is told only in `gm`, under its real name), the things lying here the POV can see (not people, vehicles, accounts or property, nor anything inside something else; at most 12, the rest counted), each with whose it is as the POV may know (its own, one lent or assigned to it to use, or another's it can see; `engine/access`), and a layout digest, the places inside it and the ways out (one route away). In `scene` and `gm`, a thing here or a way out the POV can't see, as known to whoever with it can; in `gm`, also who is in each place next door. Then its `place_lore` source (`engine/place-lore.ts`, registered after `place` so a section cut to its tokens loses lore first): each lore entry, World Info read live included (§4.2), linking the POV's place or a place holding it, nearest place first, then by priority and title, at most 6, as `Title: text` within its share (`PLACE_LORE_CHARS`, 480 characters, about 150 tokens), cut at a word keeping its start, or its end when it is pinned to one of those places with `keep: "end"`; so a long town or region entry linked from every room inside it never makes the section too big for the budget, which would leave it out whole, the description with it. It reads as the narrator may, as the prose steps do: a public entry, a Known by one the POV knows, a secret one once its gate holds, and only about a place the POV can see; in `gm`, the rest too (at most 6), marked. A line holding a World Info entry's whole text carries that entry as its source, so the interceptor switches it off for the prompt (§6.1); a cut line doesn't, so the entry still activates by its own rules for the rest, and neither does one whose text holds a macro, which the section neutralizes and the entry's own activation runs.
  - **Property** (`property`, the Economy pack, rank 12): the properties the POV holds a role on and can see, its own or a group's it belongs to (one it can't see, a faction's secret safehouse, goes only to the wider scopes), each with where it is (as it may see it), its role and share, and the other owners as it may know them. In `scene`, the properties of the people with it that they can see, told as they know them; in `gm`, every other one too, by real names (at most 10).

  Place and Property are shipped sections, ranked after the other facts so a small budget leaves them out first; a world that lists its own sections gets them only when it lists them, and the world or the GM can switch either off.
- **Who owns or may use what** (`engine/access.ts`): one reading of the four shapes ownership is stored in, for items, vehicles, property and accounts alike: an `owner` or an `owners` list (role and share, `ownersIn`, with each owner's part of the worth), a holder (`heldBy`), and a `uses` relation to the thing (with `until`, a lend still in force; without, an assignment). `owns` counts a member of an owning group; `mayUse` adds a grant in force. Vehicles, Property and the Place section's things read it.
- **Secrecy:** only the narrator's copy of a section gets scoped lines. They are in the prompt and the engine's book, so whatever in the host shows those shows them too: a wider scope is the GM's choice to put them there. Player surfaces (chips, the Journal, Play panels, the HUD, the helpers' facts) read `render`, the player scope. Every line still goes through the leak screen (`findLeaks`) except a marked line in a `gm`-scoped section. Marked lines ride after every section has its place, like hints (§6.2), one at a time while the budget and the section's `maxTokens` allow, and no size, clip or total the Context panel shows counts them; a section of marked lines alone goes in with no inspector row, and "Activation confirmed" reads only the entries with a row and the preset's own narrator rules, so a marked-facts entry the host leaves out leaves no trace there.
- **World Info sources:** a `FeedLine` may carry the World Info entry it tells (`source: {bookId, entryId}`). The turn's delivery record (`Delivered.sources`) lists those of the lines that went in, so that entry can stay out of the same prompt.

## 7. Knowledge and visibility
- **Values:** `public`; `known_by`, seen by whoever knows the subject: a `knows` relation to it, or matching its `knownBy` selector (§4.1: the members of a group, people of a kind or tier); `secret` (GM only). Entities, fields, places and tracks take `knownBy` beside `known_by`. A place may also be `discoverable`: hidden until the POV has been there, has a `knows` link to it or lists it in `known_places`, and found for good after that; its `knownBy` counts as for `known_by`. An entity may be `discoverable` too (a thing hidden in a room or a container): hidden until the POV finds it, which the built-in **Search** (`search`, `gear.search`) does by writing the POV's `knows` to every `discoverable` thing it doesn't know yet lying loose where it stands, or, with a `target`, lying directly in that container (searching or opening it; nothing in a container is found by searching the room, and nothing on anyone); found, it is known for good, and its `knownBy` counts as for `known_by`. Every other reader takes a `discoverable` entity for `secret`, failing closed. What the world document puts in a container (entities `in` it) is found as above, and a container with a `stock` category also samples a few things of it the first time it is opened, beside them: `stock` is the author asking for more. A world that wants a roll to find things authors its own `search` action, with its check and `gear.search` in its passing outcomes; it replaces the built-in by id. A reader that doesn't understand a value (an unknown string, `discoverable` on a track or a field) hides the subject, and a `knownBy` with a constraint the engine doesn't read, a malformed list or no constraint at all grants nobody: every rule fails closed, and lint flags both.
- **The POV's own observations** (`space.seen`): every place it has been, with its first and last visit (a visit counts for the places around the one visited), and where and when it last saw each person it was with and could see. It is written inside the apply of the events that move someone (a `place` set, an `in` link or unlink, a spawn, a `space.pos`), so both folds keep it alike, no turn writes an event for it and an old chat rebuilds it on replay; the start place counts as the first visit. A POV the world doesn't place starts at `start.place`. From Space 3, the POV arriving at a place with a cell stands on that cell. With the grid system on, it also keeps the cells the POV has seen on each grid (`cells[scope] = {width, bits}`: a hex bitmask by cell index with trailing zeros trimmed, started again when the grid's width changes): every `space.pos` that moves the POV adds the cells within `sightRadius` squares (at most 32) that a line of sight reaches, a blocked cell stopping the line but itself seen. The fog display reads it.
- **People on the player layer.** Scene → Present, palette targets, the narrator's people lines and the people the helpers' prompts name as present (`brief`, `outline`, `choices` and `judge`) list only people the POV can see. Cast lists the people the POV knows (met: something it owns or holds, anyone it has a relation with either way, anyone where it is, or of its region) and anyone it can see where it stands. Someone here shows what they are doing and, for an LOD1 agent, their day and next commitment; someone away shows only where and when the POV last saw them, never where they are now or what they plan. Agendas show only when public, each track on its own, so a secret one never shows behind a public one; a block of an agent's day spent on an agenda that isn't public shows as "Busy", for "their own business", in the timeline and as the next commitment. An agenda's step as someone's activity ("agenda") doesn't record which agenda it is, so Cast, the Journal and the narrator's people lines and outline read it as "busy" unless every agenda the person has is public (`castlife.activity`). Every field follows its field visibility, wherever the player or the narrator reads it: a change to a field the POV can't see (a `set`, an `add`, or a pack event that writes it, as its type's `touches` declares) is in no chip, Journal line or Montage (`eventVisible`), and the narrator's people lines and outline say what someone is doing only when the POV may see their activity field.
- **Places on the player layer.** The map, the travel list, the palette, the place ✎, the narrator's facts (nearby exits included) and the Journal show only places the POV can see. A place inside one the POV can't see is as hidden as that place, even when it is public on its own: nothing names it or offers a way there. Being anywhere inside a `known_by` or `discoverable` place counts as being there, whatever lets the POV see the room it stands in, so walking from someone's flat into its kitchen keeps both named; a secret place is never seen, not even from inside. The map still draws the places the POV is inside, but one it can't see (a secret one, or a room of one) goes by a stand-in: "Somewhere", under an opaque id, in its scopes, breadcrumbs and path, never with its own id, name or scene type, and never as somewhere to travel to. Every other player surface names the POV's own place the same way (`povPlace`): the HUD pill, Scene, the sheet, the narrator's Place line and the facts the helpers read say "Somewhere", with no id, and the place ✎ neither names it nor offers it. Cast names the place of someone there with the POV the same way, and the Garage names a vehicle's place or home base the POV can't see "Somewhere" too, while the narrator's vehicles line says the vehicle is "somewhere". The scene ✎ offers the engine's own scene types (conversation, action, exploration and downtime) and those of the places the POV can see, and no other: not one only a hidden place has, nor one the world only gives a tick or only an action sets, since a tick says nothing of who may know the type. Its "Place default" option names no other type, and a pinned one isn't marked current. The scene in force (the Scene row in the HUD details and Play → Scene, and the time controls' "now") is named the same way: any other type, whether the POV stands in a place of that type or is pinned to it, reads as the engine's default scene ("conversation"). That is display only: the clock still reads the type in force and paces by its tick, a world's own included, and Settings → Time and the time controls still list every type the world ticks, as the author's configuration. On a grid, by knowledge, the area of a place the POV can't see goes out as nothing (`void`, walled), unless the POV is inside it; in fog, the cells of it the POV hasn't seen go out blank, exactly as any unseen cell, so fog never outlines a hidden room. A route is drawn only when the POV can see both its real ends, so a hidden end is never drawn as a way to the place around it. The narrator's Place line names a place around the POV's only when the POV can see it too, and a refused narrated move names a place the POV can't see as "somewhere".
- **The place sheet on the player layer.** The place ✎ (the POV's own, titled "Where you are", and People's "Where is <name>?") opens the Character pickers' place sheet (the Character model, below) over exactly the places it offers, sent with the fix as `FieldFixTarget.places` (`knownPlaceRefs`): each with its path and parent through places the POV can see only, cut at the first around it the POV can't, so a path, a parent or a browse level never names a hidden place; and every one sent as public, so Play shows no Secret badge and tells nothing of how hidden a place is. Picking one is the same `lre.fix` as before: the one move path, its own Corrected chip and Undo; Escape closes the sheet with nothing changed.
- **Tracks on the player layer.** A secret track goes by a stand-in id wherever the player layer names it (`shownTrackId`: `~` and a hash of the world and the track), never by its own, which is the author's and may name more than any one of its truths tells: in the quest log, in the Studio's chat summary, and in the answer action's `puzzle`, which takes the stand-in of an open puzzle the POV can see. It keeps that stand-in for as long as it lasts, revealed or not, so a quest sheet open on it still finds it once its own label shows. The quest log lists its rows in the order of the ids they go by, so where a stand-in sits says nothing of the track's own id. An answer the player gives (`story.answer`'s `puzzle`, which the Answer action passes on) reaches only a puzzle in their quest log: by the id its row goes by (a secret's stand-in, never its own), or by what the player sees of it. What the player sees of a puzzle is the words its label, stage and cue share with the answer's (the lookups' words, `wordsOf`: no stop words), so a masked secret's own label never matches; words that fit none still reach the only open puzzle. A reference written as an id (one word with `_` or `~` in it) that no row goes by reads as the name of the entity or place the POV can see by that id (`study_safe`: "The study safe"), then as words. Any other (a slug such as `township_truck`, a hidden puzzle's or thing's id, one nothing has) is read by its parts alone and never as the only open puzzle: it reaches a puzzle only when its words fit one (`p_cellar_door` reaches the open cellar door). Only what the player sees decides, so an id the POV can't see (`known_by` and not yet known, or dormant) fares just as an id nothing has. `interpret`'s Answer label lists each open puzzle by its row's id, then as the row shows it ("p_study_safe: The study safe — Four digits"), a masked one, whose row reads "Something is going on" with no stage, by its stand-in and its cue, so the world id the model gives for `puzzle` is the row's. When more than one fits equally, or the answer names none while several are open, it is refused and nothing is tried, so no hint is spent. A refusal names the puzzle by the label the player sees or in their own words, and never repeats a track's own id or anything written as an id ("no open puzzle by that name"), nor a hidden track's label: those stay in the GM's reason. The world's own content answers by the puzzle's own id instead: `story.answer`'s `track`, which only an authored action or rule sets, reaches that puzzle as named, whatever the player sees of it, and its refusal names no track. A `track` given as a formula draws a lint warning (`story.answer_track`): it answers whatever puzzle the formula gives, one the player can't see too, so an action that lets the player say which passes their words as `puzzle`.
- **Player-facing views** are filtered to what the POV knows. The GM view shows everything, secrets and removed entities included, and records nothing by being looked at (L34). It is built when asked (`lre.gm`, once its spoiler gate is open) from the head's state and is never part of a snapshot or delta, so its data never travels with the player's state. It holds every entity with its fields as stored (derived ones read now), relations, placement, levels and designed sheet; every place with its visibility; every track with all its stages and truths; the ✎ fixes and GM edits in force; the held writes that didn't apply, with their raw reasons; and everyone else's money: each person at their own Money level (a band at 1; cash and one account at 2; every account and their bills from 3), with their latest history lines and payday, bill and decline notes; accounts not opened yet show the opening balances they will start from. The player never sees another person's money unless the POV holds the account. A track still on a bare start stage is dormant and stays out of the quest log until it moves on, so a quest can wait unseen for its trigger.
- **The Character model** (`shared/character.ts`, built by `engine/character.ts`): a person (an entity whose kind is or extends `person`) read as its own model for Build's Character page and GM's Character sheet, over the existing entity storage, with no world-format change. One pure builder serves both: from the draft world with no state (Build: where the world starts them; its schedules and regard read as a chat would start) and from the chat's head world and state (GM: where they are now, everything, secrets included). A draft is mid-edit, and an agent's meta write may leave its start time or epoch unparseable: then no chat could start from it, so Build's read has no schedules and reads regard by its fields like any other relation, and everything else (the places, the bed, the sections and their fields) is as ever. `lre.character {op: "get", entity, scope: "build", worldId}` reads the draft, uncommitted edits included; `{…, scope: "gm", chatId}` the head, refused for a chat no world is attached to, as `lre.gm` is (the GM gate itself is the frontend's: the sheet asks only while it is open). It answers `{character, places, things}`: every place as a `PlaceRef` (its name, the names of the places around it outermost first, its parent's id and its visibility), so pickers show paths and browse by ids; and every item (`kindIs` `item`: furniture, containers, vehicles; never an account or a deed) as a `ThingRef` with its place. The `CharacterDTO` holds the kind and tier with their labels, the POV and agent flags, a life state other than alive, its visibility, its place, what holds it (`inside`), its home, its bed (`home` when it has none of its own, else the `place` or the `item` with where it lies), the bed picker's first choices (`bedChoices`: the places in its home's subtree, nearest first, each with its path from the home, and the items lying there themselves, never carried or packed, those whose name or catalog id says bed, bunk, cot, futon, couch, sofa, hammock or sleeping bag first), its schedules in words (each one that applies to it, its own, a group's, a kind's or a selector's, with a `summary` from its days and slots, "Weekdays 6:30 AM–12:00 PM: Alder Automotive", and a `label`, its own else the summary, never its id), its relations in words (each with who it is to by name, and `words`: regard in the world's band words, any other relation's own fields with their labels; containment and the party are shown apart), and its fields in sections: **identity** (the fields a world kind in its chain authors, plus `birthday`, `occupation`, `region`, `wealth`), **where** (`place`, `home`, `bed`, `scene`), **day** (`activity`, `appointments`, `trips`, `chores`), **work** (`employer`, `pay_prefs`, `filing_status`, `local_tax`), **stats** (the sheet's numbers, as any kind in the chain files them, and `conditions`) and **advanced** (everything else, and every locked or derived field). Labels are plain words: a trailing hint in parentheses ("Birthday (MM-DD or YYYY-MM-DD)") moves to `help`, but a shape (a list's `{use, min?, …}`) is dropped, its own editor reading it; a table adds "A row of …" to `help` only when its name doesn't already say what the field is; a number keeps its `min` and `max`, which its input shows; and a list says its items' type (`of`; text when the kind leaves it untyped and every stored item is text), so a list of text is edited as its items, never as JSON. The pages show no "optional" or level notes. The model carries only what a page reads: who they work for is the `employer` field, which the page's own picker reads as a place or a business, and a relation's label and grouping are the page's, from the world. The GM's read adds `now`: the activity in words and until when, whether they travel with the POV (§9.4), and what the story holds (their place, their activity) until when.
- **Character pickers** (`frontend/character/`), shared by Build's Character page and GM's Character sheet: a place shows as its path, root first, and is picked in a sheet that matches every word typed against the starts of the words of a place's name and path (those named by every word first, then the shallower; at most `SEARCH_LIMIT`, 60), offers the page's suggestions, and browses by `parent` id, never by name (every flat has a Kitchen), opening among the current place's neighbours with each row counting what it holds. Search and browse run over the `places` that `lre.character` answers with, indexed once per sheet, with no request per keystroke. Both pages are the author's or the GM's, so every place is offered, a secret one with its **Secret** badge and a discoverable one with **Hidden until found**; no player surface uses the pickers. The bed picker writes `bed` as `bedValue` gives it (nothing for sleeping at home, the default; else the place's or the item's id) and offers home first, then `bedChoices` in its order (the rooms in their home, then the things lying there, the likely beds first), then any place, or any of `things` found by its name or where it lies. A list-of-text field edits as chips: trimmed, with a word already there in any case not added again.
- **GM edits** (`lre.edit`) change anything the GM view shows, on the head: a field (`set`, or `add` to a number, which stops at its bounds), a relation (`link` with its fields, `unlink`), where something is (`move` to a place, `put` into a container or take it out), money (`adjust`: a posting between the account and `economy.external:correction`, memo "Correction"), a track's stage, an entity's tier, agent flag and level overrides (`engine.entity`), its classes and tags (`classify`, as `engine.class`), `spawn`, `despawn` and `restore`, and `effect`: a pack effect registered for GM edits (`EffectDef.gm`), for what no entity field holds (an asset's price); any other effect is refused (`not_editable`). Each operation is checked as the effect it becomes (field types, enum values, min and max with a pool's evaluated max, relation kinds, fields and cardinality, the world's facets and their values); values are taken literally, never as formulas, and a value out of range is refused, not clamped. If one fails, the request is refused and nothing applies. A request is one `fix` post entry (its events grouped when there is more than one) marked as the GM's (`gm`, as a GM's minds run is), so undo, Keep, swipes, Re-narrate and Re-run treat it like a ✎ fix (P2, P3) while its chip reads **GM edit**, never Corrected; locked fields are writable here and only here. The chip words what the POV may see of it; when the POV sees none of it (another person's moves and doings are never worded for the player, a mind or a section's settings has no player line) it names whom it was about as far as the POV knows them (`knownSubjects`: the people and things the POV knows and the tracks it is shown, never what was set or where someone went, leak-checked), else that it happened behind the scenes. `lre.edit` answers the chip of the entry it wrote (`undo`, from the journal's `posted`: the head swipe and the entry's seq; null while it waits for a reply in flight), as a GM's minds run records its own. Once an edit applies, the GM view says what it did (a bulk change's own words, "Pinned 3 to Near", else the edit's lines) in a notice pinned above the tab bar, with an Undo of exactly the chips its own answers named, so nothing that landed meanwhile (a minds run finishing, a ✎ fix) is taken back with it; and an edit on the latest reply carries the chat's chip for it in the GM view (`GmView.edits[].undo`, null for earlier ones, which Undo no longer reaches), which the Edits log presses as the chat's Undo does. **Classify** sets the classes (any facet, hidden ones included, since the GM sees everything) and tags whole; a facet or value the world doesn't define is refused (`unknown_facet`, `unknown_class`, `single_class`, as a spawn's are), and so is one that reads as it already does. The chat holds both in the entity's state from then on, so selectors match them over the world's definition, and they count as play's for an Upgrade. The event keeps the classes it replaced (`was`), so its player line names only the facets players can see whose values changed, a cleared one as "none", the same on every replay, and never tags; an edit that changes no such facet has no player line, and its chip names only whom it was about, as for any hidden edit. **Despawn** sets the entity's reserved `gone` field, unlinks its own relations (taken out of a container, it keeps the place it was at) and everyone's relations to it except containment, so what it holds stays inside and is gone with it. A gone entity drops out of every player surface and process: Scene, Cast, the palette, the narrator's people, visibility, schedules, agents, encounters and the money processes. It matches no selector and is no group's member (`selectorMatches`, `membersOf`), so rules, schedules, `knownBy` grants and a group's regard pass it by, and a removed group's schedules route none of its members; it fires no zone or cell trigger, and `step` refuses to move it. The GM view, Restore and Upgrade read state directly, so they still see it. **Restore** clears `gone`; an agent comes back caught up, without replaying the time it was away. The POV can't be removed. Kinds and places are the world's definitions and are edited in Build, not here.
- **Batch edits.** A GM list (World, and Minds' Choose who to run) picks several at once: Select all takes what the list shows, Select a level (a button per level, never a lookalike of the list's own filters) exactly one fidelity level's characters, and its bar stays pinned above GM mode's tab bar (whose height GM mode publishes, `--lre-gm-nav-h`). GM → World's bulk change sends the existing ops, one per entity (`entity` for a tier or a domain's level, `routine_follow` for a routine's role, `mind_pin`, `mind_intent`, and `move`, the GM move as it is), in chunks of `EDIT_OPS_MAX` (64, `shared/protocol.ts`, the cap the engine and the tools share): each chunk is one `lre.edit`, all or none, with its own Undo, and the first chunk refused stops the rest. Every control starts unchosen and nothing applies until a value is picked (a value that hands a setting back, From the tier or By score, is a choice of its own), and each card shows how those it reaches stand now, from the GM view: a routine's role as this chat set it (`GmView.routineRoles`, the castlife pack's `castlife.chat_roles`), else the world's. A tier or a level goes to the people picked unless widened to everything picked; a character's change goes only to the characters picked (never the POV, which has no mind); a move to those not removed. The selection is the list's own state, behind the gate; no player surface reads it. Build's Entities and Places lists bulk-set a tier, a level override, a place's default scene type and a lore pin as JSON Patches on the draft, worked out from the draft so only what changes is written, with the same unchosen start and the same people-first reach for an entity's tier, level and pins; a map-drawn place keeps its map's scene type.
- **Life state edits** (`life`, §8): an entity's life state, the people told it, or both; everyone with the entity learns a change anyway. The GM view shows each entity's state as it is and who knows it (everyone but those whose word is older, or only those who learned it).
- **Slots** carry POV-known and public facts plus cues; truth enters only at reveal.
- **Extraction** may propose `knows` **only for public facts**: a public entity the POV doesn't know yet, or a `known_by` or `discoverable` place it can't see yet ("you hear about the boathouse"), named by id or by its name, since the prompts never show a hidden place's id; never a secret one. Secret stages advance only by predicates. The extraction and `interpret` prompts list only the entities and places the POV may know (itself, those it has met, its own, or visible where it stands; every visible one in a world that does not place the POV), and a proposal naming anything else is dropped before any change or chip; a `knows` proposal's place is the one exception, left to its channel. A proposal that carries an unrevealed secret's truth is dropped before any chip. A narration channel's refusal never tells of something the POV may not know, so it reads the same whether or not that thing exists. `knows` refuses an entity the POV may not know, a secret place it can't see (by id, or by name: a reply's name never finds one), secret lore whose gate doesn't hold and a track it may not know of in the words it uses for a fact no world has ("no world fact …"); the GM view keeps the real reason. A newcomer (`spawn`) whose name only people the POV may not know have is dropped as a change that changed nothing, applied at once or offered for Apply, with no chip and no word to the next reply, and only the log says why, as a refused move of a body the POV hasn't found is (§8); every refusal the player sees (kind, name, place, a duplicate of someone the POV may know, the spawn cap) comes first.
- **NPC decision inputs** may carry that NPC's secrets. Outputs are authored actions with observable labels; lint flags leaky labels. Secret-bearing questions never share a call with prose steps.
- **Secret lore** is for the helpers in its audience only, until its gate holds, and never for a step whose output reaches the narrator or the player; the lore search enforces both (§4.2, §5.4). The leak check (`unrevealedSecrets`) lists, besides unrevealed truths, every sentence or clause of secret lore whose gate doesn't hold, where it has at least four words and 16 characters (`lorePhrases`), so chips, Journal lines, sections, Playtest and the prose prompts never carry one word for word; an `interpret` answer that does plays as roleplay (a second action that does is dropped alone, §5.2), and a `judge` reason that does isn't shown (the roll chip and the Journal give the rating alone), unless the player typed those words. Nor is a `judge` reason that names someone or something the POV can't see (a person, a place, a thing or a track) or a secret lore entry, whatever was typed (`namesAny`): a person counts by any word of their name ("Vex" names Vex Morrow), of two or more letters in ASCII and of any length in another script, and a name of single letters (Q, M) by that capital standing alone, A and I aside since they are words; anything else, a track's label and an entry's title included, counts only by all its words in order, words like "the" and "of" aside ("under the chapel" says Under the Chapel, "under" alone doesn't). Case and the accents people leave out don't matter: a name and the text are read as written and plain, without the accents of Latin, Greek and Cyrillic letters and with ø, æ, ß and the like spelled plain, so "Zoe" names Zoë and "Soren" Søren. A word in ASCII letters counts only whole ("dangerous" doesn't name a Dan) or with an English plural ending ("the Okafors", "two lanterns"); one with any other letter counts anywhere in the reason, since a script written without spaces (李雷在监视你) or an inflected form ("Вексом" for Векс, "Łukaszem" for Łukasz) has no word end to find. A paraphrase isn't caught, which is why secret-bearing calls stay apart from prose. The inspector's lore traces leave a secret entry out entirely, show a secret-reading step's lookups only as their kind and count, and withhold any other search that carries a secret, names someone or somewhere the POV doesn't know, or names a secret entry (§6.5).
- **Player-facing wording.** Chips, Journal lines and prompts never name an entity the player has not met or a secret. A dropped or failed event is worded for the player (its player line when the POV may see it, otherwise "A change no longer applies"), and the raw reason stays in the GM view. Validator reasons name things by label (entity, place or track name), not by id. A relation refused for its kinds or cardinality (on a decline chip, or in the Outcome) names only the people the player knows, by the Journal's rule (`playerKnows`: seen and met), and anyone else as "someone"; a GM edit refused the same way is told the raw reason (`Rejection.gm`). A pack's event describer is told whom a player's line is for (`describe`'s `viewer`), so it can leave out what that POV can't see: a way opening or closing to a place it can't see runs to "somewhere", while the GM's edit lines and Check world, worded with no viewer, name it. An NPC's invitation names where to meet only when the POV can see the place, and otherwise asks the POV to "meet up", in its chip, the Journal, the Montage, the Outcome and what the narrator hears; the GM's line names the place. An encounter between two NPCs names its place only in their own record, which no player line shows. Enum values and code-like words read as words ("needs service", "on shift"). A turn's Journal entry lists the same lines as its change chip: at most 8, and when more exist, money, quest-stage and signal lines (a payday, a statement, an invitation) are kept ahead of ordinary ones and routine lines (activities, other people's moves, needs) go first; the kept lines stay in order.
- **Secret World Info entries** (§4.2) read as secret lore for the helpers, minds and leak checks of the run that read them live; in the prompt they stay stored switched off, and the interceptor (§6.1) switches one on only once its gate holds on the head state, votes every one off when that state can't be read, and leaves them off when it fails or runs out of time.
- **Acceptance:** truth text and judged paraphrases are absent from Breakdown, choices, the Journal and panels until reveal.

## 8. NPC simulation (level of detail)
- **LOD0 (every NPC):**
  - schedules: each sets one target field, such as `place` or `activity`, slot by slot, and where several set the same field the most specific owner wins;
  - event overrides until a given time;
  - seeded incidents per slot (process seeds).
- **LOD1 agents (flagged NPCs; on at every realism level, including Story):**
  - Agendas (tracks) and goals feed choices. At realism ≥3 the Cast life planner schedules whole days.
  - The engine enumerates valid actions, always including "do nothing".
  - The decision model is consulted only in the tie zone or for an invitation, and its choice is recorded in the turn's events.
  - **On screen:** intents come from public labels; the narrator reads them in the Stance section (§6.6).
  - **Off screen:** catch-up on every `slot_crossed` up to K agents per turn from a round-robin queue, in process (D9). It runs before the reply when the player's action advances time, otherwise after it.
  - **During a timelapse,** and in the catch-up for an applied `time.advance` (§9.2): every LOD1 agent steps through the interval inside the unit cap; tie-zone choices are utility-only by default (§9.2).
  - **Living the day:** in play every block of an LOD1 agent's day is taken up at its own start, as a plan's is (an agent process's moment between slot steps, §24.8), not only the block holding at each day part's end: dinner at dinner time, a fixed block at its time, a trip by its own process. When a block with a place ends and free time follows, they go home, as after a plan. The story still comes first (`routineYields`, below): a step reads where their routine had them as every block since the step before could have put them, so a block a catch-up passed over is never taken for the story moving them. A catch-up steps by slot; a turn takes the moments of an agent it has caught up.
  - **Observable fidelity in a Timelapse:** what the POV could see or meet happens at its minute, and nothing else spends events. The POV and the Live, Near and Far cast live every block at its start; a Background agent lives a block at its start only where the POV could meet them there while it lasts, or sees them leave (the POV's place read from its own day, or where the story holds it). Otherwise an off-screen Background agent lives the day at slot steps, as net state: at each step exactly where their routine has them then, so a block that starts and ends inside a slot leaves no trace, by design. Anyone whose cursor lags behind the slot a run reaches (one the turns before didn't step through) first steps through the slots before it, so their own moments in it happen at their minute as everyone's do, and a skip's first slot never passes over them. Both folds replay the run as recorded.
- **The player's character** follows its own routine (place and activity schedules, and its day plan when it is an agent) only when time runs on its own (a timelapse: the palette's, whatever its target, a run to a routine point, a stated time, an activity's Speed through), and never against what the player or the narration set: a span the player's own words spend doing something (a typed out-of-character span, "OOC: for the next two hours, I service the fleet", or a length stated to an action whose Speed through runs it, "sleep for eight hours", `TimelapseOptions.povRoutine` false), a narrated skip (an applied `time.advance`) and the clock's ✎ fix keep it where the story has it until the span is over (`AdvanceOptions.povRoutine`). An ordinary turn leaves it where the player took it. A narrated move across a gap that no single-mode route spans is declined ("There's no way from … to …"), and a POV that is stranded, or whose routine's place cannot be reached, stays where it is.
  - **Its Continue:** the stop or cap chip of a span the story held keeps the hold (`povRoutine` false on the chip's action), so the rest runs as the span did.
- **Where a character's day comes from** (`shared/routines.ts`, `packs/castlife/core/routines.ts`), most binding first:
  1. **What the story established:** a place or activity an action, the narration's read or a GM edit set; holds (a trip, `castlife.override`, a story plan's block under way); the GM's intent (`mind_intent`, §24.9).
  2. **Commitments:** schedules (shifts, school, the world's and the chat's own versions), appointments and trips (`commitments.ts`).
  3. **Story plans** (the memory step's and the GM's), laid into their windows (§24.8).
  4. **Their routine:** the world's routines (`world.routines`, one item per routine; Build → Routines) that cover them, owned as a schedule is (an entity, a group, a kind, a selector, or none: the world's default), the most specific owner's block winning by block id (their own, a group's, a kind's, a selector's, the world's; equal precedence linted, the lower routine id winning), and a routine with `inherit: false` cutting the less specific ones; on top, the chat's own changes (`routine_block`, the GM's or the story's). A block is **fixed** (at a time), **flexible** (a length somewhere in a window, or `perWeek` times a week, a floating block) or **optional** (as flexible, by its `chance`, the first thing a busy day leaves out), with its days (weekdays, days off, holidays, birthdays, seasons, weather), place (home, work, here, with its company, a place, the nearest place of a scene type), company (and whether it asks them along), priority, needs and source (authored, a lore entry or a sheet field with its quote; in a chat, the story with its turn or the GM).
  5. **Basic needs:** sleep, breakfast, lunch (at work, else home) and dinner (`BASIC_BLOCKS`), each replaceable or turned off by a routine. A character with no routine at all (`routine_follow` mode `off`) plans from the story, their commitments and these only.
  6. **What the world grounds otherwise:** their chores (authored), their agendas' steps (story tracks) and getting back what an overdue loan keeps.

  Nothing else fills a day: no outing, gathering, holiday or birthday party, and no invitation, that nothing in the world or the story grounds. The occasion-shaped template earlier builds planned every day from (sleep, meals, seeing friends, time out, holidays, birthdays) plans no new day; it is kept only for folding what those builds recorded (below).
- **Routines are defaults, never authority.** A routine or a schedule never moves anyone out of a scene with the POV, never moves a Live or Near character (their mind and the scene speak for them: a routine is only a soft suggestion), never makes an invitation that interrupts for them, and never stops a time skip for them. The scene with the POV is the one the story holds: an ordinary turn and its catch-up, a typed span, a narrated skip or a ✎ fix (`SlotInfo.povRoutine` false) keep whoever is in it there; while time runs on its own the POV follows its routine and no scene holds anyone, so in a Timelapse a housemate still goes to work and a trip still leaves. Otherwise it yields to what the story set (`routineYields`): someone the story put elsewhere than their routine last had them stays there, between the routine's own edges always, and at an edge as the world's **awareness** (`routineSettings.awareness`, per character in a chat with `routine_follow`) says: `story_first` keeps it until the story or the GM releases it, and Background and Far characters bend for story plans and the GM's intent; `balanced` (the default, the precedence by fidelity) keeps it until their night (the edge into their sleep block, or a schedule's first slot of a day), Background characters keep their routine and schedules, Far ones bend for story plans and the GM's intent; `routine_first` lets the routine's next edge take them back. A trip waits until they're out of the POV's scene. Schedules ask Cast life the same (`castlife.yields`).
- **Routine roles** (issue #48): routines and schedules inform; they don't dictate. Each level of closeness has a role (`routineSettings.roles`), and the GM can set one per character in a chat (`routine_follow` `role`; null returns them to their level's): **informative** (the default for Live, Near and Far) is context only, what their mind (the `routine` read source, §24.5) and the narrator may consider: the planner and their own place and activity schedules never move or occupy them, in a turn or in any skip, with the POV or away from it, and their routine asks no one along; **enforced** (Background's default) is the planner living it, every story-first rule kept; **off** is not even context. A level is read as it is now (§24.1), with no mind asked. What isn't routine still runs whatever the role: their story plans (after one, someone whose routine isn't enforced stays where it left them, at rest), trips, the GM's intent, and the world's schedules and money (weather, prices, business hours, bills, paydays: a job pays its scheduled shifts though the worker's place isn't enforced). An enforced Live or Near routine still never stops a skip for them: their arrival where the POV is only notes (the signal's `stops: false`). A trip's end, like its start, takes no one out of the scene the story holds with the POV: the trip still holds them, and they go home once the scene lets them go. Older chats replay what they recorded; the roles reach a running chat from its next turn.
- **Skip lives** (issue #53): how someone whose routine is informative spends a time skip (a palette Timelapse, a typed span or a narrated skip; `SlotInfo.timelapse`) is their level's choice (`routineSettings.skips`) or the GM's for them (`routine_follow` `skips`; null returns them to their level's): **stay** where the story has them; their **routine**, lived for the skip as an enforced one is (`routineLived`), except where a story plan, a trip, the GM's intent or an appointment holds them; or **model**, the blocks the model planned for them at the skip's start, recorded as `castlife.skip_plan` ahead of the timelapse and kept whole in the castlife slice (`skipPlanOf`), lived for its span, each block at its own minute; one already under way as the skip starts (planned, or a basic need below) at its first minute, a run's opening moment (`AgentInstant.opens`), never at the first slot's step. Only someone the engine lives a day for is planned (an LOD1 agent from Cast life 3, as for a story plan, §24.8; `livesPlans`), so no plan is made that nothing would live. **Basic needs are the floor:** any minute of the plan's span that no block of theirs covers (between its blocks; all of it for someone it left out, and for everyone when its call failed, timed out or was cut off) they keep to their basic needs as their day lays them (`dayPlan`'s sleep, breakfast, lunch and dinner, or a routine's own block with the same id: sleep at home at night), at rest where one leaves them until their next block, and nothing else of their routine (`skipBlocksOf`). A basic need whose place is `work` (lunch, by default) is had where they are: the floor never sends anyone to their workplace. So they go home to sleep on their own, and the plan never has to name a home, which the POV may not know (a secret one is never offered). A story plan, a trip, an appointment or the GM's intent still comes first. By default Live, Near and Far are planned by the model and Background (when informative) follows their routine. In a skip, and only there, a block their skip life gives them (planned or basic) may take them out of the scene the story holds with the POV and later bring them back: the POV sees them go (an `engine.note` the Montage tells, seen as their arrival is, by a POV that can see them and has met them, whether or not it holds a `knows` link), and their arrival where the POV is only notes and never stops the skip. The turn's record keeps each person's net place, as every timelapse's does (§4.3), with where the POV saw them in `engine.observed`. Under stay nothing moves them, and outside skips nobody is moved into or out of the POV's scene. An enforced routine is always lived and an off one never. A replay reads the recorded plan and never asks again, and a Continue inside its span lives it on.
- **Tuning** (`world.routineSettings`, Build → Routines → Settings; absent, the defaults): `awareness`; `invitations` (`off`, `player`, the default, or `everyone`: a fixed block that asks its company along lays the block in their days too, when they know the host and follow their routine with invitations); `sociability` (`reserved`, `usual`, `outgoing`: how fast an NPC's social and fun needs grow, half, as they are, half again); `planBend` (`must`, `strong`, `standard`, the default and the rule of §24.8, `ordinary`: which story plans take their time from work and school); `roles` (above); `narrator` (on by default: present characters' usual routines go to the narrator as soft context); and `paletteFollowsRoutine` (off by default: where the palette Timelapse's Follow my routine starts, §9.2).
- **Writes are shape-checked** (`backend/worlds.ts`): a routine whose shape the planner can't read (`routineShapeProblems`: a block's kind, `at`, window, minutes, `perWeek`, chance, priority, days, place, company, needs or source) and routine settings with a setting or value the engine doesn't take (`routineSettingsProblems`) are refused before anything is written, by Build's put and set and by a patch alike (the outside tools' too), with a message naming each field at fault. The planner skips a malformed routine and reads a bad setting as its default, so a save the check let through would change nothing unseen. What a routine names that the world lacks (a place, an owner who matches nobody, a lore entry) stays a lint warning after the write.
- **A chat's own changes** are GM edits (§24.9), each one `fix` entry with its Undo: `routine_follow {entity, mode?, awareness?, role?}` (mode `follows`, `no_invitations` or `off`; role `informative`, `enforced` or `off`; null returns a part to the world's), `routine_block {entity, block, value}` (a block, `{id, off: true}`, or null to drop the change) and `schedule_override {entity, schedule, value}` (`{off: true, until}` or their own `{slots, recurrence?}`, ranked as their own; null drops it). They record as `castlife.follow`, `castlife.routine` and `castlife.schedule`, each carrying the change whole (an earlier build's `castlife.follow` has no role and folds as it did). The world's routine data is only where a chat starts.
- **The story's own changes** come from the reply's read (the `routine` channel, §9.4, `packs/castlife/core/story.ts`): a reply that states a lasting change to someone's week records it as the same `castlife.routine` and `castlife.schedule` events, with source `story`, its turn and its clock, through the `castlife.routine` effect (which a rule or an action may use too, as what its story establishes). A new job, shift or habit is a block of theirs (`story_<its words>`, or a basic need's own id when it moves one, priority 4, so it outranks the world's defaults where they overlap but never a block that must happen), at the place the reply names (else their work for a job, else wherever they are, so it moves no one the reply didn't); a habit they gave up turns off their blocks it names (by name or place) and drops their schedules' slots at the place it names, with their activity schedules' slots at the same times; a move to a new home sets `home`, which every block at home and every schedule's rest place follow. A block the story adds is theirs from the day after its turn, since that day was already laid as the story had it (and its night brings no morning before it); what the story ends ends at once. The GM view lists them among the changes the story made, with their turns; the chip's Undo, or a GM edit of the same block or schedule, takes one back; and as every post-reply record, a Re-narrate keeps only what its own reply's read gives, and a Re-run reads again (§5.1, §4.3).
- **The player's message as a source** (`backend/routine-ask.ts`, §9.4): a routine the player states in their own message ("I start nights at the marina Monday", "Marcus says he's started swimming on Tuesdays") is never played. `interpret` reads it beside the message's actions, in the `routine` channel's shape with the words that state it, and the chat asks on that message, "Would you like to add this to your routine?" (or "to Marcus's routine?"), naming who, what, when and where, each once (a place or a person the label already names isn't named again). The turn doesn't wait on it. **Yes** records it through the `routine` channel and the `castlife.routine` effect, checked against the chat as it stands then, with source `story`, the message as its turn and `by: "player"`, from the next day as the story's changes are, written as a confirmed entry on the head with an **Undo** chip, and pushes the state again once that chip is in, so GM mode, which reloads its view as the chat's chips change by id, shows it at once; **No** drops it, and nothing applies without the yes. GM → Routines lists it among the story's changes by the block's own name (`lre.routine {op: "changed"}` carries each chat block's `label`, so a block not yet in force never reads as its id), as "From your message" with its turn, where a reply's change reads "The story" (`storySourceLabel`). Its bounds are the channel's (the person exists and the POV may know them, the places exist and the POV may know them, the days are stated, nothing private ends, one already in their routine or schedule changes nothing) and more: the words are in the message and a statement, never a question; the person is the POV or someone the message names; nothing carries an unrevealed secret. A refusal at Yes says only that it changes nothing now: why stays in the log, since a refusal may know a secret.
- **The read model** (`lre.routine`, `packs/castlife/core/view.ts`): for the GM, a character's effective routine (each block with its owner, source and what it shadows), every schedule that applies (which wins, which are shadowed, the chat's change), their day as laid with why each block is there (`BlockOrigin`), what the story holds now, and the invitations they made and received with their grounds; for the player, only what Cast shows of someone the POV may know (present, an agent, Cast life 3), each block with why as the POV may know it (a lore entry only when the POV may read it; a private block or a secret agenda "Busy"; never a GM change, a place or company). Cast's timeline carries the same `source`.
- **Replay.** A turn's events are recorded, so a fold never re-runs the planner: what an earlier build's planner laid stays as recorded, and the new defaults reach a running chat from its next turn, with no Upgrade. The one exception was `castlife.need`, whose apply read an NPC's day plan for the need's base; new need events carry that base (`from`), and one recorded without it folds against the earlier template (`legacyDayOptions`) at the rates earlier builds fixed in code, whatever the world's needs settings now say, so older chats fold as they did.
- **Needs** (Cast life 4; `shared/needs.ts`, `packs/castlife/core/index.ts`): hunger, fatigue, social and fun, each from 0 (met) to 100 (urgent). Each goes by one name everywhere, with one narrator's word and one line of what meets it (`NEED_WORDS`): Build's page and hint, a routine block's needs, the sociability lines, Play's Needs, Check world and the narrator all read them there.
  - **Settings** (`world.needs`, Build → Needs): per need, `on` (off: it never grows, shows, is told or alerts), `rate` (its growth an hour, 0 to 100), `tell` (from here the narrator's People line names it and Play marks it, 60 by default) and `alert` (from here it raises the `needs` signal in a timelapse, 80 by default; the interrupt policy says what that does). Absent, every need is on with the rates earlier builds fixed in code: hunger 4, fatigue 3, social 1.5 and fun 1 an hour, and sociability still scales an NPC's social and fun. A write is refused when it names no need, no setting or a value out of range (`needsSettingsProblems`), and stores only what differs from the defaults; Check world flags the same, and an alert set below the point the need is told at (`castlife.needs`).
  - **Build → Needs** (`needs-editor.tsx`, its own page): per need an On/Off in its header, what meets it and the narrator's word for it, then each number said in words with its value ("Grows by 4 points an hour, out of 100", "Told to the narrator from 60", "An alert from 80"), how long it takes from met to each point at that rate, and that the alert stops a skip only as the interrupt policy says. Off shows no numbers. Saved whole (`lre.editor set part needs`), and **Use the defaults** removes the world's settings.
  - **Play → Me → Needs** (`castlife.needs`): under a note that each runs from 0, met, to 100, urgent, each need's gauge, and under it what its number means and what meets it: the point it's told from, in the world's own settings ("From 60 you're hungry, and the narrator is told"), or once it's pressing that the narrator knows, then "Met by eating" (the view's `tell` and `metBy`).
  - **The player character's** needs are recorded (`castlife.need`, from 20 at the world's start) and shown on Play → Me → Needs (`castlife.needs`, from Cast life 4, each need the world has on, pressing from the same unrounded value and point the narrator's line reads, so the two never disagree). What meets one meets it at its **end**, so the need grows again only from then: a meal (`eat`, half an hour), a rest (`rest`, half an hour, fun; its activity `waiting` meets fun), a stated nap (`sleep` with `minutes`, fatigue) and a chat (`chat_with`, a quarter of an hour, social), each at the end of its own time. A chat is relationships' action and needs nothing of Cast life: it gives the speaker the activity `chat` for its span, and Cast life's rule `castlife.conversation`, on the regard a conversation makes (`why` `conversation`, `CONVERSATION` in `shared/needs`), meets the POV's social need (`castlife.company`): at once for a conversation someone else begins, and for the POV's own chat at its span's end, which the turn reads as any activity's (Ordinary turns, below), or at once while the clock is paused or a fight holds it, since no turn then reaches that end; so a world without Cast life chats as before. A rest's or a sleep's span that passes within the turn is met by the action at its end (`castlife.satisfy` with the activity, which checks the Speed through policy); one the turn offers or runs as a Timelapse is met as it is lived through, and a Speed through declined meets nothing: the next turn that lets no time pass ends the span (§9.2). A night's sleep is met as it is slept through: a skip or a speed-through (the POV is stepped in each) meets the needs of what the POV lived by each slot's end, its sleep, meals and time out (`povLivedNeeds`: the activity of the block of its day holding then while its routine runs, else the span the action that began it gave it, `activity_until`, while that is still the activity, `activitySpanEnd`); a moment its routine moves it from one activity to the next meets what the one it leaves met (`povActivityEnds`); and where the span its action gave it ends inside a slot, or the run itself does first (a speed-through to 07:00), a moment then meets them at that end (`activityEndMoment`). Someone it knows (seen and met) with it at a slot's end meets its social need. Only time the run itself passes counts (`SlotInfo.run`): the slots a skip first catches the POV up through, and any part of a slot before the skip began, were played in ordinary turns, so what the POV did then is the story's and never read from now. Going to bed and getting up again meets nothing; an activity the story left behind long ago meets nothing either.
  - **Ordinary turns:** each turn's own time is read as it ends (`ProcessDef.lived`, called by a turn-mode `advance` for the POV; Cast life's `povTurnNeeds`): someone the POV knows (seen and met) with it then meets its social need, in `castlife.need` events carrying their `from`.
