An activity whose span (`activitySpanEnd`) ends within a turn's time meets that activity's needs at that end, read by the turn after each action's time (`ProcessDef.ended`, Cast life's `povSpanEnded`), and world rules hear those needs as they hear any: one an action of the turn began (a world's own dinner action, a nap or a rest passed in the turn, a chat, a bath a later queued step sees out) as part of that action, so "Not an action?" and a claimed thing's Undo take the need back with the activity, while the time and the company it gave stay; and one an earlier turn began that is short enough to pass in turns (under its Speed through threshold, or with no policy: a bath the world gave none, the last minutes of a night), unless an action has set another activity first. One its turn offered a Speed through to is lived only by a time skip: the next turn that lets no time pass ends it unmet (§9.2), so going to bed and getting up again meets nothing, and nor does a nap turned down; an activity whose span ended before meets nothing. The story holds the POV in a turn, so its routine says nothing here; catch-up never reads the POV, and a skip never reads time played before it (`SlotInfo.run`), so no time is met twice. What the turn's own time gave is marked `lived`: the turn's bookkeeping, which Play → Me and the narrator's line read and no "what changed" line words, so a day with friends doesn't say "social eased" every turn; what an activity met is worded as the activity's. Records from before this reading fold as they did: the events are new ones, made only as a turn is planned.
  - **Stated lengths:** eat, rest, sleep and chat take `minutes` (a `duration` param, `STATED_MINUTES`), which interpret fills only from a length the input states ("rest for an hour" is 60; the deterministic read takes it from the typed words, `typedSpan`, never the first number). A meal and a chat take it as their time. A rest and a sleep keep their own (half an hour; going to bed, 5 minutes; a shorter stated length, that), and the stated length sets when their activity ends (`activity_until`; without one, a rest's half hour and 07:00), so the user's Speed through policy carries the rest of it (§9.2): under its threshold it passes within the turn ("sleep for 20 minutes" is a 20-minute turn, "rest for an hour" an hour's), past it the turn offers a Timelapse to that end or, under `auto`, runs one, and the next turn that lets no time pass ends the span unmet. A stated span is the player's own words spending the time: its Timelapse holds the POV in it to the end (`TimelapseOptions.povRoutine` false, carried on the chip and its Continue), so no routine wakes a daytime sleeper for dinner; going to bed for the night follows the routine, and the rest of a night under the threshold passes in later turns, which meet it as one sees it out.
  - **Beds:** a person's optional `bed` is a place, or an item, wherever it is (`bedPlace`). A sleep block at home happens there (`blockPlace`), and Go to bed takes the sleeper there (`castlife.to_bed`, through the one move path, `relocate`; nothing without a bed or when they're there). Build's person editor (**Bed**) and the GM entity page's Placement (**Bed**) pick it. A bed is a field like any other on player surfaces: a secret or hidden place it names is never told there, and the planner's player lines never name a place.
- **Life states.** Every entity has one: the built-in `alive`, `dying` and `dead`, or one the world defines (`lifeStates`: a label, whether someone in it still acts, and how the narrator reads it, "lies dead"). An entity starts in its own `life` (alive when unset) and play changes it only through `engine.life`, which the `life` effect writes (`{entity, to?, tell?}`, for rules and actions), as do a GM edit, the `life` narration channel and combat's down rule (`ruleset.combat.down.life`: falling into it, alive again once back up from it). `engine.life` declares the state touched, so an Upgrade keeps it (§4.3).
  - **Stillness.** Someone whose state doesn't act follows no schedule and no day plan, takes no trip, meets no one, is asked no `npc.choose` (day-plan ties and fight turns alike), steps no more as an agent, on screen or off, and rolls no initiative and takes no turn in a fight. An agent who acts again comes back caught up, as a restored one does.
  - **The body stays.** Unlike Remove from the world, the entity keeps its place and relations: Scene lists it, the palette offers it, selectors match it, and the narrator reads it where it lies ("Sir Alaric lies dead.").
  - **Who knows** is the engine slice's `life`: per subject whose state changed in play, what everyone took it to be before and what each person who has learned of it since last learned. It is written inside event apply, so both folds keep it alike: a change teaches everyone in the subject's place; `engine.told` teaches the people a rule, a GM edit or a reply names; and every event that moves someone teaches whoever it leaves in a place with the subject, someone walking in or a body carried in. Cast, Scene, the narrator's people lines, the outline, the helpers' prompts and the Journal read the state the POV last learned, so a death it hasn't heard of shows nowhere and a revival it hasn't seen still reads as death, with no hint; the GM view reads the truth. A timelapse whose coarse record would teach differently keeps its full events.
  - **What the player reads of it.** A change records who saw it (`witnesses`: everyone in the subject's place as it resolves), and only they get its line, so a death learned of later is the telling's news and the Journal never dates it. Other people's place and activity changes, and their changes to any field a schedule sets, never read in the Journal, the change chip or a Montage: the Journal words past turns against what the POV knows now, so it can't tell which of someone's comings and goings the POV saw, and a known person who died unseen would simply stop appearing; Scene and People say who is here. People voices no regard for a body the POV knows of; Stance and `agents_present` read who acts from what the POV knows; `invited(host)` is false for a host the POV knows is dead, and nobody invites a POV that doesn't act. A reply can't move someone who doesn't act, nor anyone the POV knows only as a body, and what the POV knows decides how the move is refused. A body the POV knows of is declined in a plain chip, in what the POV takes it to be ("Sir Alaric lies dead; that move didn't happen"), even one since brought back where the POV didn't see, so the chip never hints at a revival; someone the POV takes to act who doesn't is dropped as a change that changed nothing, applied at once or offered for Apply, with no chip and no word to the next reply, and only the log says why, since a refusal no living person gets would tell of a death the POV hasn't heard of. Neither, nor a move no route spans, is worded as a declined payment.
  - **Starting knowledge.** An entity can name who knows the state it starts in (`lifeKnownBy`, entity ids); everyone else takes it for alive until they see it or are told, so list anyone who starts in the same place. Unset or empty, everyone knows.
  - **Time skips and Upgrades.** A timelapse reads who still acts at every slot, so someone who dies part-way stops there, their cursor at the last slot they lived, and a two-day skip ends as two one-day skips do; catch-up passes over anyone dead or removed. An Upgrade that brings an agent back to a state that acts (its own life state, or a world state that now acts) catches it up from then. A life change the player hasn't heard of goes to a hidden row of its own in the Upgrade preview, and hidden rows sort after every shown one.
- **Group chats:** character entities link to Lumiverse character ids.

## 9. Granularity and domain packs (D17, D18)

### 9.1 Two dials

**Depth per domain** (realism levels 0–4):

| Domain | 1 Narrative | 2 Simple | 3 Detailed | 4 Simulation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Money** | Wealth band | Cash plus one account (ledger) | Accounts, cards (with autopay), recurring income and bills | Interest/APR, statements, minimum payments, loans, investments, withholding |
| **Gear** | Notable items (authored ones marked `notable`, and everything that comes into play: found, bought, handed over or claimed) | Items, containers, cash as currency in a wallet | Worn/equipped slots, kits (template plus overrides), condition | Wear per use, consumables |
| **Vehicles** | "Runs fine" | Fuel %, location, owner | Specs, consumption, odometer, use grants, home base | Maintenance intervals, seeded breakdowns, registration/insurance, marine and off-road specifics |
| **Cast life** | Day-part routines; agendas as tracks | Commitments set the day | Day planner | Needs/drives, invitations, NPC↔NPC encounters, knowledge spread |
| **Relationships** | One band per pair | One number per pair | Multiple axes plus appraisal rules | Decay, history, group regard |
| **Space & travel** | Named places | Places plus routes and travel times | Grids, positions | Terrain multipliers, mode-specific routes |
| **Calendar & time** | Day parts | Clock and dates | Recurrence, time zones | Holidays, terms, exceptions |
| **Weather** | Season mood | Climate states | Regional climates | Microclimate effects on travel and rules |
| **Sheets & checks** | Bands as words | Stats plus one check | Skills, derived fields | Full sheet |

Level 0 turns a domain off. Story systems (tracks) do not depend on realism. Time controls and Timelapse (§9.2) need Calendar ≥1; at level 1, targets round to day parts.

**Tiers and bands.** A world names its own tiers (`WorldDoc.tiers`: label, band, order, an optional agent default and description), and each maps to one of three fixed **detail bands**: `principal` (shown as Leads), `supporting` (Supporting) and `background` (Background). Caps are per band and domain. A world written before tiers existed gets three tiers whose ids are the band ids, so its entities, settings, `resolve` markers and stored ledgers read unchanged.
- **Effective level** = the per-entity override if set, otherwise min(domain level, the cap of the band its tier maps to). An override can never exceed the domain level. The tier is read through the world each time, so a tier's band follows the revision in force. An entity's tier that the world doesn't define keeps its band when it is a band id (one spawned before the world renamed its tiers), else runs at the domain level; lint flags unknown tiers.
- **The POV** is `world.pov` and is never capped, whatever its tier. A legacy `pov` tier on the POV is moved to the lead tier (the lowest-order Leads tier) when the revision is read; revisions are immutable, so this happens on every read.
- **New entities:** a spawn that names no tier of the world gets the world's spawn tier (`spawnTier`, by default its lowest-order Background tier), and an entity without its own agent flag takes its tier's agent default (never the POV).
- **Realism presets** (`Custom` allows any combination):

| Preset | Money | Gear | Vehicles | Cast life | Relationships | Space | Calendar | Weather | Sheets | Band caps (Leads / Supporting / Background) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Story** (default, D22) | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 1 | Domain levels for all |
| **Grounded** | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 3 | 2 | 2 | none / none / 1 |
| **Simulation** | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 3 | 3 | 4 / 3 / 2 |

- **New chats start from the world.** The starting markers (§4.3) carry the world's own realism (its levels and band caps, a band it caps being its whole say for that band) and time controls; the user's settings fill in what the world doesn't set (the time scale, and any domain, scene type, interrupt class or activity the world leaves out). With "Use my settings instead of a world's defaults for new chats" on (off by default), the user's realism and time settings replace the world's instead, and a world cap the settings don't keep is lifted. Later changes are new markers. A level is a whole number from 0 to 4; anything else reads as 0.
- **Player claims are layered the same way** (§9.4): what a typed action's thing the world doesn't have becomes (`real`, Make it real; `ask`, Ask first; `hold`, Hold me to the world) and whether Make it real asks first above the world's claim cap (`capped`). A new chat's starting `claims` marker carries the world's `realism.claims`, else the user's **Settings → Realism → Player claims**; with "Use my settings" on, the user's. Each realism preset carries its own (Story: Make it real at any price; Grounded: Make it real up to the cap; Simulation: Ask first), so picking one sets it, and changing it makes the preset Custom. A running chat changes its policy with a later `claims` marker from **GM → This chat → Realism**, applied from the next turn; a chat started before claims existed plays by the world's, else by what its own realism preset carries (Story's when it is Custom), as a settings file saved before claims reads (`claimsOf`). Build's **Realism defaults** set the world's with the same control (off, the user's fills in), and lint flags one the engine can't read. The cap is the economy pack's `claimCap` (whole minor units, **Claim cap** in Build → Packs → economy beside the narrated-price cap; empty, `derivedClaimCap`, about the price of a meal), and one that isn't a positive whole number is the lint warning `economy.claim_cap`, the engine then rounding a positive fraction and reading anything else as unset. Money is never made by a claim.

### 9.2 One representation, gated by level
- **Same data at every level.** Levels gate which processes run, what panels and slots render, and what extraction may propose. They never change the data shape.
  - **Money:** one double-entry ledger whenever an entity has numeric money (≥2).
    - Level 1 renders a band from net worth.
    - Entities without a ledger keep a band field.
    - Changes made at level 1 post as balanced adjustments against the **external economy** counterparty.
  - **Kits** are a template plus sparse per-piece overrides. Taking a piece creates one override item, and completeness is derived.
  - **Fuel and odometer** are tracked as numbers from level 2.
  - **Decay and needs** are computed closed-form at read time.
- **Promote and demote.**
  - Promotion switches processes on. An entity that lacks representation gets it through lazy materialization.
  - Demotion switches processes off and narrows rendering. The data stays live (nothing goes dormant), so re-promotion never restores stale detail.
  - The demotion preview lists predicates whose inputs will freeze.
- **Level contract per pack:**
  - the fields that are live at each level;
  - **level-independent query functions** for predicates, the planner and renderers (`wealth(x)`, `can_afford(x, amount)`, `has_item(x, kind)`, `fuel_ok(v, route)`, `regard_band(a, b)`);
  - **cross-pack services** with defined behavior at every level. For example, `economy.charge` is a no-op at 0, a band check plus adjustment at 1, and a posting from 2. Similar services cover `gear.transfer`, `vehicle.consume` and `relationship.appraise`.
  - **Mixed-level parties.** Every party posts at **its own** effective level. A counterparty with no numeric money (level ≤1, or Money off) is represented in the ledger by the **external economy** account, tagged with the counterparty's id, and its band moves by its level-1 rule. Money is therefore always conserved in the ledger, whatever the mix of levels.
  - Cash is a currency stack in a wallet container when Gear is 2 or higher, otherwise a plain cash account.
- **Lazy materialization:**
  - **Materializers take the entity's current representation as input** (band, notable items, current aggregates, known facts) and sample **within** it. A level-1 NPC moved from "poor" to "rich" materializes a balance inside the "rich" band, never a value drawn from the seed alone.
  - **What counts as "show":** a **player-facing observation or an in-fiction action** (opening a trunk, emptying a wallet, auditing an account). Showing a pack's Play panel is an observation of the POV: switching to the tab sends `lre.view.open` for that panel with no entity, which materializes the POV in the domain of the panel's pack. The inspector, audits, playtests and Dry Run render **labelled previews from the seed** and record nothing; the GM view shows what is recorded and, for what isn't yet, its representation (an unopened account's opening balance), and records nothing either. One more thing needs a person's money: the recurring money processes (Money 3+) open the authored accounts of the people they act for, posting their opening balances (the same event a view records; it draws no randomness) in the first slot holding 09:00, so their bills draw on the money the author gave them.
  - **Until it is materialized, an entity shows its representation.** For money that is each authored account's `opening` amount, exactly what the materialization posts: the POV's Money panel and the HUD cash show it (net worth and band included), as the GM view does for everyone else, so a new chat never shows zero and nothing changes when the accounts open. A ✎ cash fix materializes the holder's money first and measures its correction from there, recording both in the fix.
  - **Where it is recorded:** materialization during a turn goes in that swipe's `events`, ahead of the action's effects. Between turns it goes in the head record's `post`. **Inside a turn's in-flight window it is deferred** (P2, §4.3): the panel keeps showing the representation (or "pending", for detail that has none, such as a container's contents) and the detail materializes on the head once the window closes, so nothing shown is ever re-sampled.
  - It is capped per write and recorded with `generatorVersion` and `tablesRevision`. Detail materializes once per entity and domain: a materialization of detail already recorded fails its check, so a carried, kept or held entry holding one leaves it out and keeps the rest, re-checked without it (a ✎ cash fix whose accounts a Re-run's reply already opened still sets the cash), and an opening balance never posts twice. An entry left with nothing to apply is dropped or refused with its note.
  - **Pinned versions.** Each world revision pins the generator versions and data-table revisions it uses: tables are copied into the revision (§4.1), and every pinned generator version stays in the bundle. A running chat keeps its revision's pins across extension updates. New tables or generators reach it only through **Upgrade** (a `migrate` marker), never silently. First-shown detail stays recorded in `events`/`post`.
  - Detail whose source materialization entry is missing from the current fold (a branch cut before it, a Discard, a drop or an inert entry in a rebased view, a Re-narrate drop) materializes again from the same seed and pins when it is next shown, but from the current representation, so it may differ.
- **Time (D29).** Deterministic by default; no time control or timelapse needs a model call.
  - **Every turn has an elapsed time:** a timelapse segment or the action's duration, otherwise the scene's default tick × the time scale; zero while the clock is paused. The Scene slot states it ("This turn took N min." or "No time passes this turn.").
  - **Time controls.** A new chat takes the world's scene-type ticks, interrupt policies, Speed-through policies, montage and unit cap as starting `time` markers (§4.3), with Settings → Time filling in what the world leaves out (always the time scale), or replacing the world's with "Use my settings" on; Play → Calendar changes the running chat. A mid-chat change to any time control is a `time` marker (§4.3): a pure intent with `seq`, journaled and folded like a realism change.
    - **Default tick per scene type**, editable: conversation 2 min, action 1 min, exploration 10 min, downtime 30 min; worlds may add types and set their defaults. A tick is 0 to 1,440 minutes. Settings and time markers, the starting markers included, round it to the nearest whole minute, so a tick under half a minute plays as 0 and its turns take no time; a world's own tick for a type no marker sets (with "Use my settings" on, a type the user's settings don't list) is read as written. The time scale multiplies the tick after that, so a turn's elapsed time can be a fraction of a minute ("This turn took 0.5 min."). The scene type is the POV's `scene` field, set by actions, rules or a ✎ fix; while it is empty, the place's (or an ancestor's) default applies.
    - **Time scale:** a global multiplier on default ticks only (0.25×–10×, default 1×). Action durations, travel times and timelapses stay exact.
    - **Clock paused** for the current scene: zero elapsed time (ticks and action durations) until resumed or the scene changes. Timelapse asks to resume first. Pausing again after a scene change restarted the clock holds it in the new scene: a pause marker drops the old scene's pin, and the next plan pins the scene it finds. The HUD, the Timelapse sheet and the Scene section read the pause as the plan does (`clockPaused`). A time skip the reply narrates still applies (§9.4).
    - The interrupt policy, Speed through, Montage granularity and the timelapse unit cap (below).
    - **Hard maxima** hold wherever a value is read, so a world document, an imported ledger or an intent can never ask for an unbounded run: a tick is at most one day; the time scale stays within 0.25×–10×; a timelapse reaches at most 366 days from now; a Speed-through threshold is between 1 minute and 7 days (one range, `SPEED_THRESHOLD_MINUTES`: Settings keeps a stored threshold inside it, rounded to the minute, the time controls' stepper offers exactly it, and markers and the engine clamp to it).
  - **Skips without asking** (`UserSettings.time.askBeyondMinutes`, Settings → Time): how long a skip runs inside a turn without asking, one user setting for both channels, a day (1,440 minutes) by default. A duration the player types ("for the next three hours") and a jump a reply narrates ("three hours later") apply only up to it; a longer one becomes the Timelapse chip, never run unasked, so a misread number can't skip days. It is whole minutes from 0 (every skip asks) to the 366-day timelapse reach, its only ceiling: a stored value past the reach keeps the reach, and one that isn't a number of minutes reads as the default. Unlike the time controls above it seeds no chat: every chat reads it as it plays, from its next turn. An *until* target (a part of the day, a shift, a commitment, a meal), a stated day and time, a palette or chip timelapse and the clock's ✎ fix are the player's own explicit targets and never ask.
  - **Timelapse** (palette sheet, Calendar panel or chip; §11):
    - **Targets:** a duration (minutes, hours, days, weeks), or *until*: next morning or another day part, the POV's next shift or commitment, arrival (the active trip ends), a date and time, a track reaching a stage, or "until something happens" (the first `stop` interrupt). "The next <day part>", whether in the parameters or in the player's text, means the following occurrence when the coming one is under three hours away, so "sleep until the next morning" at 07:14 sleeps the night; a "tomorrow" only the player's text holds (the parameters having dropped it) reads the same way, while "tomorrow evening" in the read's own words is that part tomorrow (typed time, below).
    - **It is an action:** a user message with `intent:{timelapse, target, overrides}` and `triggerGeneration`. Per-run policy overrides travel in the intent.
    - **Simulation:** the engine run steps the interval in process (D9), slot by slot with process seeds (so it equals N one-day steps), inside the unit cap. The POV follows its own commitments and routine like an LOD1 agent, so pay, bills, commutes and fuel post as they fall due. **Following its routine is the player's choice from the palette:** the sheet's Follow my routine (`followRoutine` on the request, opening as the world's `routineSettings.paletteFollowsRoutine`, off by default); off, the skip holds the POV where the story has it, as a typed span does (§8), and records that hold on the message (`povRoutine: false`), so a Re-run and its Continue keep it. Money posts as it falls due either way, and the world's schedules run.
    - **Interrupt policy,** per class `stop` | `note` | `ignore`: invitations, bills, declines, incidents, quest triggers, rule triggers (rules may be tagged `interrupt`), NPC arrivals at the POV's place, overdue use grants, needs thresholds (Cast life 4) and weather alerts. Defaults: `stop` for invitations, declines, incidents, quest triggers and arrivals; `note` for bills and paydays, rule triggers and overdue use grants; `ignore` for needs thresholds and weather alerts. After each slot step, in slot order, the first `stop` event the POV can see (`signalVisible`) ends the timelapse at that slot's end; `note` events go to the Montage. A signal the POV can't see never stops one, as it never stops an action queue (§11): the early stop, its chip and its Outcome line would give it away. The same state and policy give the same stop. A turn that stopped early says so ("Timelapse stopped: <reason>") and offers to continue.
    - **Unit cap** per segment (deterministic; it can be lowered but never raised): 600 agent-days (LOD1 agents plus the POV, × days) and 5,000 events. A longer request ends its segment at the last whole slot inside the cap, **always at least one slot**, and shows a **Continue timelapse** chip with the time left. Settings and lint reject a cap smaller than one slot for the world's LOD1 agents plus the POV. Seeds do not depend on segmentation, so segments compose exactly when nothing else changes state between them (a timelapse turn proposes no `time.advance`; below).
    - **NPC choices:** tie-zone choices use utility only. Besides a time skip's minds (§24.7), the one model call a timelapse may make is the **skip plan** (§9.4): as a span starts, the informative people whose skip life is `model` (§8) are planned by one helper call, recorded ahead of the timelapse, and a Continue inside that span reuses the plan rather than asking again.
    - **A timelapse that can't run** (the clock is paused, the target has passed, lies more than 366 days ahead or doesn't exist, there is no upcoming shift, commitment or active trip, or Calendar is off) simulates nothing; the turn records only a note saying why ("Timelapse not run: …"). The simulation is bounded by the unit cap, never by the wall clock; a context handler the host gives up on leaves the turn unplanned, and it commits `degraded` (§5.1).
    - **Recorded** as the turn's `events` plus a summary event `{from, to, target, stoppedBy, segment}`. Derivable events go in as per-period aggregates and period closes (§4.3, record size). Re-run reproduces the interval (process seeds, utility choices); Re-narrate copies it.
    - **Not a model channel:** an explicit timelapse is user input and is not bounded by the `time.advance` cap. Model-proposed advances stay capped (§9.4).
  - **Montage slot** (engine book; filled on timelapse turns; ranked after Outcome on the section budget):
    - key events, filtered to what the POV knows (§7);
    - aggregates: money in and out per account at the POV's Money level, distance and travel, the bands crossed in fields the POV may see of people it knows (regard, sheets, needs: "calm → frantic"), and net changes to the POV's and its belongings' other numeric fields it may see (a banded field is told by its band, never its number); at most 6 band lines and 4 change lines;
    - granularity: key events only (default), per day or per week; coarsened to fit the budget;
    - default line: "Narrate the interval from the Montage; don't add events.", which reads "Don't add events." when the Montage has nothing to show (the Outline points only at sections the narrator has, §9.5). The Journal keeps the full list.
  - **Speed through,** per activity type, when the activity's remaining span exceeds its threshold. New user settings give the four listed activities (sleep, work shift, travel, waiting) `offer` past 30 min; a settings file saved earlier keeps the policies it has and isn't refilled. A world may set its own for any activity. An activity without a policy (in a chat started before it had one, a world's own activity the world leaves unset, or one the user's saved settings don't list) is never sped through, however long its span: a length the player stated runs on within the turn (below), and otherwise the turn elapses only the action's own duration and the rest of the span passes in the turns that follow. Its controls say so ("never sped through: its time passes in your turns") until a mode or threshold is picked, which gives it a policy. Actions declare the activity they start and its span (until 07:00, the shift's end, arrival). The span is recorded with the activity it was given for (`_activity_span`) and read only while that is still the activity (`activitySpanEnd`): a meal's `set`, a narrated move, a routine or a ✎ edit that sets another activity leaves the new one no end of the old one's, while a GM's edit of "Activity ends" stamps the activity as the whole edit leaves it, so Activity and Activity ends set together read together in either order. The engine's own records of a span (`ENGINE_FIELDS`: that one and `_activity_offered`, below) are never listed by a view, a mind or a prompt.
    - `offer` (default): the turn elapses only the action's own duration, and a chip offers a Timelapse to the activity's end, recording the end offered (`_activity_offered`). The offer goes at the next send, so the next turn that lets no time pass is the decline: as it begins (`turnStart`, `lapsedSpan`), it ends the span where it stands (`activity_until` set to then), and nothing of the span is met. Any time skip carries it on instead, the chip's Timelapse, its Continue, the palette's or a typed one, since skipping time gets no one up; a time skip's pre phase starts from the same state the plan does (§24.7). A span no turn offered (one short enough to pass in turns, or one begun while the clock was paused or a fight held it, which the turn offers nothing for) is never turned down; a Continue or an Impersonate plans no turn, so it ends nothing.
    - `auto`: the turn runs that timelapse itself, with the interrupt policy and a Montage.
    - Activities first seen by extraction always get the offer chip, since the engine never starts a generation on its own.
    - A span at or under its threshold gets no offer, as one without a policy: a stated length runs on within the turn (below), and the rest of any other passes in the turns that follow.
    - A length the player stated to the action (a `duration` param, §8) is theirs, all of it carried past the action's own time: under its threshold, or with no policy, the turn runs on to its end; past it, its Timelapse, offered or run, holds the POV in the activity to its end, as a typed span does.
  - **Typed time.** The action catalog always includes "Let time pass": its `span`, required, the input's own words for how long or until when ("over the next hour", "later that evening"), with minutes, until a day part, shift or commitment, or at a stated day and time. **A time skip is that action, whatever else the input says** (§5.2): an out-of-character instruction that time passes or skips ahead ("OOC: over the next hour, I sort the stock", "(OOC: skip ahead two hours)", "[OOC] later that evening"), or in character a span spent on something no catalog action covers. What it says happens in that time fills it, and the reply tells it: the turn runs the skip through the same time skip as the palette's Timelapse (§24.7), never the activity it describes at that action's own duration.
    - **The span is read from the player's words, the same way every time** (`typedSpan`, `engine/time.ts`), and only once `interpret` has chosen to let time pass, so it never decides whether a message is a skip: a length ("over the next hour", "for the next two hours", "half an hour", "an hour and a half", "three hours later"; one gone by, "two hours ago", or a rate, "every hour", is none); a vague length, by a fixed table (`VAGUE_SPANS`): "a moment", "a few moments" and "a few minutes" are 5 minutes, "a little while" 15, "a while" 30 and "a few hours" 180, so "a few minutes later" skips 5; a part of the day ("skip ahead to evening", "until the afternoon", run as the Timelapse sheet's Until runs it; "later that night", "this evening" and "tonight" only while that part is still ahead today; "the next morning" as for any target; "tomorrow evening" that part tomorrow, where before the day's first part it is still the night before); an anchor of the POV's own day ("until dinner", "until lunch", "until breakfast", "until bedtime", "until morning"), which runs to where its day plan has it, the next start of its meal block or its bed today (else tomorrow), and for the morning the end of its night's sleep, else, with no such block, to the calendar's matching part of the day (evening, midday or afternoon, morning, night); or the next shift or commitment ("until my next shift", "till work"). Words that also name a day and time ("Friday at 6 PM") are left to `at`, and words that name no span ("for some time") or two different ones read as none.
    - **The target** is the first of: the read's `span`; its `at`; its `until`, in the player's words ("later that evening") or as a day part, shift or commitment; its `minutes` (a number written as text counts). A read that chose the skip keeps it however it gave the span: one split off into `then` is read the same way, and with none in the read, the message's own span when it states exactly one. With none at all the turn plays as roleplay, and the log says the read let time pass but stated no span it could read. A message the read gives no skip is never made one by the time words it holds ("I'll be back in an hour," I tell Danny).
    - Its description tells `interpret` the day and time as the HUD shows them, in the POV's place's time zone ("it is now Mon 2026-09-14 06:45"), so "skip ahead to Friday at 6 PM" can be read as `at: "2026-09-18 18:00"`, and `then` may carry an `at` too. The engine reads it with the world's calendar, as local time at the POV's place, into an `at` target. Only `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM` (or with `T`) naming a day the calendar has, at a time from 00:00 to 23:59, is a stated time; anything else is none, and the other parameters are read instead. A stated time that has passed, or lies beyond the 366-day timelapse reach, is refused like any target the timelapse can't reach: the turn runs none and records the note ("Timelapse not run: that time has passed"). A duration up to how long a skip runs without asking (a day unless Settings → Time says otherwise, above) runs as the turn's own timelapse; a longer one is offered as a Timelapse chip and never run unasked, so a misread number can't skip days. An *until* or a stated time isn't capped: "until my next shift" runs as the turn's timelapse however far off the shift is, inside the unit cap. A span typed in character after other actions ("…then wait until morning") is offered, not run, once they have all played; a stated time is offered as "Timelapse to Fri 18:00?". A chip names its time as the HUD does, local to where the POV is, so in Chicago a typed 18:00 reads 18:00.
  - **Extraction** proposes a bounded `time.advance` as **all** the time the reply narrates for the turn ("an hour later" is 60), never less for what the turn already took, and the engine keeps only what goes **beyond** what the swipe has already applied (the turn's elapsed time), never less than nothing: after a 15-minute action, "an hour later" advances 45 more, and a narrated total under 15 advances nothing. The netting is the engine's (`extract`), not the prompt's, so the action's own time is never counted twice, whether the advance applies automatically or on a confirm chip's Apply. Records written before keep the advance they recorded. It proposes none on timelapse or Speed-through turns.
    - **Under the cap** (each proposal up to how long a skip runs without asking, above: a day unless Settings → Time says otherwise; §9.4) the advance applies together with its **catch-up**: the interval runs whole, like a timelapse segment (every LOD1 agent inside the unit cap, every interrupt class at `note`; the narration told the span, so the story holds the POV and its scene, §8), so it equals a timelapse to that time the story holds the POV through. Under `auto` it is computed at once in the post pass and stored with it; under `confirm` the post pass checks it the same way and offers a confirm chip, and Apply runs it from the head's clock and stores it in the confirm entry, paused clock or not, as `auto` does, against the setting as it stands then. It is all or nothing: if the interval exceeds the unit cap or the catch-up cannot finish within the post-pass bound, the advance is not applied and becomes the Timelapse chip below. **During a fight** (`fightHoldsClock`) none applies in either mode and none is offered: the post pass declines it with a decline chip, and the next reply is told so; Apply on an earlier chip is refused while one is under way (§11).
    - **Over the cap,** up to the 366-day timelapse reach, or when its catch-up cannot apply whole, it becomes a **Timelapse** action chip to that time, with every interrupt class at `note` (§9.4). A skip beyond 366 days is dropped, with no chip. A turn already played keeps what it applied: the setting reads at the post pass, so changing it changes only the turns after.
  - Recurring processes step per slot with process seeds. Closed form is used only where it is exact.
  - **The clock's ✎ fix** (§12) sets a date and a time of day, read in the world's calendar as local time at the POV's place (as typed time is), never before the story starts, with Calendar ≥1. It is one `fix` entry, so Undo, Keep, swipes, Re-narrate and Re-run treat it as any fix (§4.3), and the next plan starts from it.
    - **Later than the clock:** the time passes as an applied `time.advance` to then does (L28): the interval runs whole, every interrupt class at `note`, with no Montage. It is the player's own input, so how long a skip runs without asking doesn't bound it, only the 366-day timelapse reach and the unit cap: past the unit cap nothing applies, and the refusal points to Timelapse, which gets there in steps. During a fight (`fightHoldsClock`) it applies nothing, as for a narrated skip, and says why: the fight's rounds take no clock time. Its events are recorded coarsened, as a timelapse's are, and as one group, so a rebased view, a held write or a Re-run keeps or drops it whole.
    - **Earlier than the clock:** one `engine.rewind` event sets the clock back, and the engine slice keeps how far it had run (`reached`; a second set-back inside the window keeps the furthest). Nothing that ran up to there is undone, and none of it runs again (L48): until the clock passes `reached` (`ranTo`), every way time moves (turns, Timelapse, an applied advance, the ✎ again) moves only the clock. No slot ending by then is stepped again, so no world process (flows and bills, markets, weather, upkeep, invoices, the day planner's settling) and no slot or day rule or track step runs twice; no schedule edge or agent's own moment by then fires again, so no move or appointment replays; the agents' catch-up cursors already stand past it; and a time skip finds no beat there, so no mind thinks there again. What reads the clock against a recorded time (cooldowns, consolidation, a Far mind's refresh) waits until the clock passes that time again. Past `reached`, the world steps as it would have.
  - **Property:** skipping N days equals N one-day steps and any segmentation of the same timelapse.

### 9.3 Packs
Each pack ships a pure `core` (kinds, relations, level contract, services, query functions, schedules and rules, actions, effects, materializers, data tables) and a `ui` (renderers, Play panel, editor views), plus lint, tests and loop-sim scenarios.

| Pack | Contents |
|---|---|
| **Calendar & time** | Gregorian and custom calendars, weekdays, holidays, terms, recurrence, per-place time zones, the per-turn tick; **time controls** (ticks per scene type, time scale, clock pause, `time` markers); **Timelapse** (targets, interrupt policy, unit cap, segments); **Speed through**; the **Montage** slot (§9.2). |
| **Space & travel** | Places, routes with modes (walk, road, off-road, water), distances, terrain, grids, positions, tracking. Reachability is a query: a move across a gap no single-mode route spans is declined. |
| **Gear & containers** | Own, hold, wear and store; equip slots; containers with weight and volume limits; stacks; kits as template plus overrides (a piece named in free text matches by its words in any order: "metric combination wrench" finds "combination wrench, metric"); condition, wear, consumables.<br>**Use grants** (lending): a loan ends at the time the player says ("until 7 tonight", "for 3 hours", "tomorrow morning", "Friday"), which wins over the days; words that name no time fall back to the days, and with neither there is no loan. When a loan runs out the thing stays where it is, is flagged *overdue*, and the owner's planner schedules retrieval. A thing handed back to its owner, or given to a new owner, ends its loans and clears *overdue*; passed on to someone else, it stays lent. |
| **Vehicles** | Road, marine and off-road kinds; fuel type, capacity, consumption by mode, terrain and load; range; odometer or engine hours; condition; maintenance; status; location and **home base**; owner; **assigned to** (a use grant with the same expiry semantics); passengers and cargo as containment; refuel, repair and service through `economy.charge`; seeded breakdowns. |
| **Economy** | The money foundation (§25): parties holding accounts through owners and roles; products as data with class traits; cards; flows for every scheduled movement of money; one statement cycle for every credit product; a journal with holds; credit reports and scores; payment preferences; cash machines and branches; overdraft policies; households; catalog and category-band pricing. Work, payroll, businesses and taxes are its work module (§26); holdings, prices, property and net-worth sources its holdings module (§27). Money levels and the features each turns on are §25.12. |
| **Cast life** | Commitments (jobs, school terms and timetables, appointments, trips and vacations, chores); needs and drives; agendas; a day planner (deterministic utility, seeded tie-breaks, recorded tie-zone decisions, re-planning on interrupts); off-screen execution; NPC↔NPC encounters. |
| **Relationships** | Per-pair multi-axis regard; appraisal rules; closed-form decay; bands that feed the planner and narrator voice; history; groups (factions, families, households, companies) with membership roles and group regard; kinship (`parent_of`/`child_of`, `sibling_of`, `spouse_of`, `partner_of`). |
| **Sheets & checks; Weather; Story** | §3, §8, §10. |

### 9.4 Every way a model can change state, with its bound

| Channel | Bound |
|---|---|
| `interpret` → action and parameters | Validated by the action's requirements; an action whose ref param names nothing that fits, as the POV knows it, is dropped with a decline chip saying why, and the message's other actions still play (§5.2). A thing the player says they have that the world lacks is a claim instead: made (never money, never an authored id), asked about first, or dropped, by the chat's Player claims policy, with an Undo (§5.2). Each queued action likewise, played in order against the state the one before it left; the queue stops at a check, a failure or an interruption (§11), and what it leaves is only offered. "Not an action?" undo. |
| `interpret` → a timelapse target | A model-read duration longer than the user lets a skip run without asking (Settings → Time, a day by default, §9.2) isn't run; it becomes a Timelapse chip. An *until* target (a part of the day, the POV's next shift or commitment) or a stated day and time isn't capped: it runs as the turn's own timelapse, inside the unit cap, however far off it is. A stated day and time counts only as `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM` naming a day the world's calendar has, read as local time at the POV's place; one that has passed or lies beyond the 366-day reach is refused with a note (§9.2). The span is the player's own words, read the same way every time (`typedSpan`, §9.2), and only once the read has chosen the skip: the reading never decides whether a message is one. Activity spans are engine-defined, so Speed through `auto` may still apply. The plan is made after `interpret` (§5.1), so the turn simulates the action it read. |
| `interpret` → Try something (an inferred check, §11) | A skill the world's sheet has and the chat's policy allows; a difficulty band clamped into the policy's bounds; companions and opponents the POV may know, and present when it plays; under `tries` the read must say the input tries, under `risky` its chance against the band must be under the adjudication policy's `riskyAt`; at most the policy's rolls a turn; dropped after a step with its own check in the same read. It rolls with the world's own checks under the adjudication policy, recorded; opponents' and companions' numbers stay out of every player view and the narrator's unless the policy shows them. |
| `extract` → `time.advance` | All the time the reply narrates for the turn, of which only what goes beyond what the swipe has already applied (the turn's elapsed time) is proposed, never less than nothing; none on timelapse or Speed-through turns. Up to how long the user lets a skip run without asking (Settings → Time, a day by default, §9.2) a proposal, whole, with its catch-up: applied in the post pass under `auto` (its starting mode), or on a confirm chip's Apply under `confirm`. Longer, up to the 366-day timelapse reach, or when the catch-up cannot run whole, it becomes a Timelapse chip with every interrupt class at `note` (§9.2), in either mode; beyond 366 days it is dropped, with no chip. During a fight it never applies, in either mode: it gets a decline chip ("Didn't apply: +N min", a fight is under way, with Remove it and Accept) and an Outcome note for the next reply (§9.5), adds nothing to the changes chip, and a confirm chip's Apply is refused. A paused clock holds ticks and action durations, not time the reply says passed, so under a pause Apply does what `auto` does. Explicit timelapses (palette, chip) are user input, not this channel. |
| `extract` → spawns | At most the world's spawn cap a chat (`spawnCap`, set in Build's World part: a whole number from 0 to 500, 24 when absent; 0 lets no reply introduce anyone), deduplicated by name against the people the POV may know; a name only someone it may not know has is dropped unseen (§7). The kind and place are validated. A newcomer's id is its name's slug and a digest of the name and how many replies introduced someone before it (`npc_vex_morrow_1f3a9c2e`), never the name alone, so it can't be an authored id; the `spawn` event stores it, so replays and swipes fold alike. |
| `extract` → `knows` | Public facts only: a public entity, or a `known_by` or `discoverable` place the POV can't see yet (by id or name); never a secret one. Anything the POV may not know of is refused as a fact no world has (§7). |
| `extract` → `life` | Someone died, is dying, collapsed or came round, named by a person the POV may know and a life state by id or label. Judged by what the POV knows: what it already takes to be so is refused (`already`), and a state it hadn't heard of is no refusal, so nothing is given away. Applying it tells the POV: the change, or, when it was already so, only that. |
| `extract` → `routine` | A lasting change to someone's week the reply states (§8): `start` a new job, shift or habit, `stop` one, or `move` to a new home. Read from the reply alone, as every channel, so never from a mind (no mind reaches a helper, §24.3) nor from the player's own wishes. Its person is one the POV may know who acts and whom the reply speaks of: the POV, or someone it names (the read checks the reply's words); its places and people are ones the world has and the POV may know (one it may not reads as none). The read is asked to put any place the reply names for it in `place`, as a place id or, when nothing lists it, in the reply's own words ("the community pool"), so a place the world doesn't have is refused as there's no such place rather than left out; one the POV may not know is refused the same way, so nothing tells of a secret place. A change with no `place` stays placeless, as before: a habit at no named place ("reads every evening") is wherever they are, and the label's words never make or refuse a place. It needs the days the reply gives (weekday names in the calendar's own words, weekdays, days off or daily) and takes only the times it gives (a time, or a part of the day; none: a floating block in the waking day). A habit only mentioned, already their routine (a block by that name on those days at that time) or their schedule, changes nothing; a fixed block that clashes with one of their schedules applies only when the reply says it takes the place of what they did then (`replaces`), and then takes them off each such schedule in the same change, so an authored schedule is never contradicted unsaid. What it ends is only what the reply can know: never a private block. Every refusal is unseen (logged, no chip, no Outcome note): a routine is a soft default, so the next reply is never told something didn't happen. The change is the chat's own on top of the world's, with source `story` and its turn; a block it adds is theirs from the next day. GM → Routines badges whom it changed by where the changes came from: "Changed by you", "Changed by the story" (a reply), "Changed from your message" (`by: "player"`), or the ones that apply together; a change not in force yet reads "In force from the next day", whatever its source. |
| `interpret` → `routine` | A lasting change to someone's week the player's own message states (§8): read in the `routine` channel's shape with `said`, the words that state it, and never played. It is asked about on the message ("Would you like to add this to your routine?", Yes or No) and applies only on Yes, through the `routine` channel, so every bound above holds, with source `story`, the message as its turn and `by: "player"`. Unlike a reply's, a place the player names that doesn't settle on one still asks (`placesFor`, among the places the POV may know only: the place by id or whole name, else each place whose name's words start with every one of theirs): several fit and the chip offers each ("At Lakefront Park", …, then No), the one chosen recording the change there; none fits and it asks with "No place you know of fits "…", so it would have no set place.", Yes recording it placeless. A place the world lacks and one the POV may not know read alike, so a secret place is never offered or told apart. Never asked: words the message doesn't hold, a sentence that is a question ("do you still swim on Tuesdays?"), someone the message doesn't name (other than the POV), a person or a place id the POV may not know of, an unrevealed secret, or what is already their routine. Undo takes it back. A Decision route's read has none. |
| `extract` → NPC presence or location | Story first: a narrated move lands whether or not a route joins the places. Only the world's travel simulation (`mapSettings.travelSimulation`, §4.1) declines a move no route reaches. A move to another place leaves behind what they were doing where they were (the shift their routine or the planner gave them): their activity goes back to `idle`, unless the proposal gives one (`activity`), and a stated activity holds even for someone already at the place (the party brought them first). Schedules aren't consulted for anything else: a routine moves no one by itself. To tell where a move the reply describes is going, extract is given the usual routines of the people with the POV (`usualRoutineForExtract`: §6.10's projection with each person's and place's id, apart from every fact, whatever `routineSettings.narrator` says; a world's extract or effects template reads it as `{{usual}}`): "heads out to his usual lunch spot" at noon names that place's id, or `usual` when the reply doesn't say which, and a move to `usual` goes where their routine has them now (`usualPlaceNow`: the place of today's clause under way or next that isn't where they are). It then reads as any narrated move, by the location mode. One whose clause is a private block, at a place the POV can't see, or of someone whose routine role is `off` resolves to none and is dropped, and no later clause stands in for it. The read also says where the reply leaves the POV (`where`, asked while `location` is a channel), since a reply can take a move as given without telling it ("at the counter with Marcus" while the engine has the POV at the market) and no change would say so: a place the engine doesn't have them at, nor one around it, is offered as their move there, unless the turn's own events moved them (the engine's outcome wins) or a narrated move of theirs already says where. A place the reply only implies is weaker evidence than a told move (a flashback, a phone call, a look across the street, a misread), so it is a guess: whatever the location mode, it waits on a confirm chip whose Apply plays the move, never applies by itself, and is no outcome for the type's learning (§11), so guesses never move told moves toward `confirm`; one that can't happen is dropped with no chip and no Outcome note, since the reply never told it. Under `never` (the player's pin, or a locked place field) it is ignored, as any move is. A change under `confirm` that is refused (any channel) gets the decline chip and Outcome note an automatic refusal gets, never only a log line, except one refused unseen and one naming what the POV may not know (`proposalKnown`), which stay silent. ✎ Place (`lre.fix` on `place`) is a real move, as the GM's Move: out of whatever holds them, onto the arrival cell, with their activity back to `idle`; the same ✎ on someone in People ("Where are they?") moves them, and either takes only a place the POV may see, for someone it can see (anything else is refused as no such place). **One move path** (`engine/relocate.ts`): every writer of where someone is (the `move` effect and so Go and a narrated move, driving's driver and riders, routine blocks and skip-plan blocks as castlife takes them up, trips, `castlife.override`, schedule `place` edges, the GM's Move and a field-editor `set` of `place`) asks `relocate` for the move's events, so each leaves the same state: an `unlink in` per holder, `set place`, and the arrival cell (`space.arrival`, the POV from Space 3), in the event shapes rules, sightings and replay read; a narrated move (`told`) adds the activity rule and, under the travel simulation, the route check (`no_route`). **The party**: a person linked `travels_with` the POV (the GM's **Travels with**, an edit with its Undo) who acts and is in its scene comes along on every move the POV makes to another place: through the `move` effect, when it drives (as a rider), and when its own routine blocks, schedule `place` edges and trips move it (a Timelapse that follows its routine). A "move" to where the POV already is (✎ Place, the GM's Move, a narrated move that stays) takes no one. It never takes someone the same read or edit already put somewhere other than the POV's place (`movedElsewhere`, read from the Working's events: their flat inside the building the POV stands in is still `together` with it), so the proposals' order doesn't matter, and one the reply moves after the POV goes where the reply says. Whoever comes along (a party member, a Go companion, a rider) leaves what they were doing behind (`idle`). Go's `with` (a list of person refs, which interpret fills from "with X" and "we", and the Actions sheet from its **Who comes along** switches, `PaletteEntry.people`) brings the people it names who may come along (`companionCandidates`: in the mover's scene, seen by it, acting, and for the POV only someone it may be told of, `povMayKnow`, so an unmet stranger in a room inside its place, whom Scene → Present withholds, is never offered by name); anyone else named is dropped without a word, so a hand-made request can neither bring nor reveal someone hidden. |
| `extract` → purchases and transfers | Priced from catalog or category bands; bounds per field; declines (§9.5). A transfer's two sides are people or their own cash, checking, savings or card accounts (§9.5). |
| `extract` → `give` | A handover between two characters, the recipient real. The item is an id, or a name: the one thing the giver holds by that name (any case, without a leading article) is handed over, so "she hands him the brass key" moves the key she carries rather than making a second. A name that fits none or several is read as before: something only an NPC can hand over untracked, which comes into being held by the recipient (`gear.receive`, notable), while the POV can't give what it doesn't have. A cash give is a transfer (§9.5). The prefix `extract` reads names each thing's holder, one the POV may know, and lists the scene's people and things first and money accounts last within its 30 entities. |
| `extract`'s input | The reply, the state before it (`prefixSummary`), and the player's message it answers (the pre phase's text; none on a read without one, such as a Continue), given as context only: the strict rules still take changes from what the reply states happened, never from what the player asked for. |
| Stacking | A thing bought (`economy.buy`) or handed over untracked (`gear.receive`) joins the stack its holder already has rather than making another entity: an item they carry, in a bag or not, neither worn nor gone, that the POV can see (a hidden one is never joined, so nothing vanishes into it), with the same owner and the same catalog row (else, with no row, the same name), whose `qty` grows by the quantity. Recorded as an `add` of the quantity (a `set` only when no count is stored), as `use_up`'s count down is, so the entry folds right over another parent (§4.3); older records replay as they were. |
| Applied from a chip | A change the player applies on a confirm chip (Apply, or a purchase's price, item or Pay another way) is recorded with what follows from it on the head, in the same `confirm` entry (§4.3): the rules its events fire and the tracks it moves (`stepTracks`), as an automatic change's post pass does, so a quest item taken on Apply moves its quest that same turn. A narrated time skip's catch-up ran its own rules, so only its tracks are stepped. |
| `extract` → items (`take`, `drop`, `use_up`, `wear`, `lose`, `item_condition`) | What the reply says happened to a thing, each naming who (`entity`, the POV when left out) and the thing by id, or by a name: the one thing by that name they hold, or (for `take`, `wear` and `item_condition`) lying loose where they stand. Only things the POV can see count; a hidden or undiscovered one reads as none, so its refusal tells nothing. `take`: lying where they are and on no one (that is a give or a theft), not theirs already. `drop`: on them; it lands where they stand. `use_up`: on them, a whole number from 1 to 10 at a time (`qty`, 1 when not said); the last of it leaves the world (`gone`), holder and owner kept. `wear`: on them or within reach; `off: true` takes off what they wear (`gear.wear` and `gear.remove`, so slots and gear levels hold). `lose`: on them or theirs; with `by`, stolen into the thief's hands, still the owner's; else lost (`gear.lose`). `item_condition`: a whole number up to 25 a reply, clamped to 0–100. All start at `confirm`. |
| `extract` → regard | A reply moves each axis of a pair by at most 10 in all, net (a proposal without an axis moves every axis). In the reply's order, a later proposal for the pair is cut down to what is left; one with nothing left is refused like any change past its bound (an automatic one gets a *Didn't apply* chip and its Outcome note). |
| `extract` → condition, quantities | A condition needs a person and a name. A quantity changes by a whole number up to the item kind's `qty` bound (10 by default); a larger step is refused. |
| `npc.choose` | Picks only from engine-enumerated valid actions; recorded. |
| Skip plan (`castlife.skip_plan`) | Spared helper calls as a timelapse span starts, planning how the informative people whose skip life is `model` (§8) spend it, as a few fixed blocks of place and activity each, only for what they do other than simply being at home asleep: gaps are fine, since any minute no block covers is their basic needs (§8), so it never plans hour by hour or names a home. The world's `routineSettings.skipPlanCalls` says how it asks: `together` (the default), one call for everyone; or `parallel`, one call per person, all at once, each with the same instructions and only that person's input, so no answer grows with the cast × the span, and their checked answers merged into the one event. It is told only what the POV may know: the span; each person's place when the POV may know it, else "somewhere of their own"; their activity when the POV can see it; their usual routine as the narrator reads it (`knownUsual`, never a private block); the story's appointments, trips and plans in the span, leak-screened; the places the POV can see; the world's activities; the latest reply (bounded) and the Journal's recent lines, never a mind's. It shares the `skip` step with the beats' call (§24.7), so it reads that step's pins as the beats' call does, read as no one (a secret entry stays out), within the pinned lore limit minds read (§24.5); and, while minds read Recent chat (§24.5), the chat before the skip's turn within that row's limits, less the latest reply it reads already, each line leak-screened as the Journal's are. Routines are context, never rules. A block holds only when its person was asked about, its place was offered, its activity is the world's, it lies inside the span without overlapping another, and its label passes the leak checks (`findLeaks`, hidden names); the rest are dropped and logged, and anyone left with nothing keeps to their basic needs. Recorded ahead of the timelapse in the same swipe, so a replay, Re-run or Re-narrate reads it and never asks again, and a Continue inside the span reuses it. Each call runs on the `skip` step's own settings (Settings → Steps and connections → Time-skip beats: its connection, model, max tokens and sampling, else the helper model's), with the preset's `RPEngine · skip` block (§6.8), and its answer is bounded by that max tokens. On a call's failure, its timeout (Settings → Timeouts, Time skip plan, which each parallel call runs inside) or an answer cut off at that limit, no one that call asked about is planned and they keep to their basic needs; the event carries a note for the GM that counts them (how many of how many, never who), and the calls are spared: the turn's run doesn't halt on them, and the skip still runs. |
| Prompted `effects` | Same validation and bounds as extraction. |
| `puzzle.answer` | Authored opt-in per puzzle; a threshold on a probability. The puzzle is named by words from its label, or left out when exactly one is open. |

Everything else (balances, prices, interest, payroll, fuel, travel, time ticks, timelapse intervals and interrupts, weather, schedules, planner days) is engine-only.

**Extraction quality was measured on a deliberately weak model** (DeepSeek V4 Flash), and the numbers are a floor, not the design target (D31). The first measurement, on 38 labelled narration fixtures, gave precision 0.41 with the base prompt and 0.74 with strict rules, against a 0.90 target. Two corrections followed:
- **strict prompt rules:** `time_advance` only for an explicit jump or stated duration; `knows` only for public facts the POV newly learns; `regard` only when the prose states a change; `give` only for a handover between two characters; never a duplicate;
- **deterministic dedupe in validation:** a purchase subsumes the seller's handover and its payment transfer; a cash `give` (only when the whole item is money) is a transfer with method "cash"; a payer must be an account.

I then re-measured with the engine's own `extract` step as shipped (strict rules plus dedupe; the same 38 fixtures and scorer; 38 calls): overall precision **0.68** (30 of 44), recall 0.71, and no forbidden proposals. Per type: `time_advance` 1.0 (6/6), `condition` 1.0 (1/1), `regard` 1.0 (4/4), `transfer` 1.0 (2/2), `spawn` 1.0 (1/1), `give` 0.71 (5/7), `location` 0.67 (2/3), `purchase` 0.56 (5/9), `knows` 0.44 (4/9), `quantity` 0 (0/2). The samples are too small to move a cold-start mode, so D4's defaults stand; learning on the player's own connection (§11) is the gate.

### 9.5 Narrator contract
- **Facts are authoritative.** The sections hold the established facts, and each line that reports what happened says how to tell it: an Outcome line ends "Narrate this result." (or "Narrate the attempt failing.", and the like), and a timelapse's line ends "Narrate the interval from the Montage; don't add events."
- **The Outline points only at sections the narrator has** (`backend/section-refs.ts`). The outline step is told the sections the turn will fill (the Outcome, the Scene and the Outline, a timelapse's Montage when it has lines, Minds when a mind has a line, any other section whose renderers have one). As the Outline is written, in rank order, every clause of a line that names a section the narrator doesn't have goes (a sentence's parts between semicolons; a sentence or line left with none goes whole): one ranked before the Outline that wasn't filled (empty, or clipped by the budget), one the world has none of, and Minds when no mind's line fits. A section is named by its label after "the" ("the Montage") or before "section" ("the Minds section"). This holds for the engine's beats, the brief and the outline step's beats, and for a kept plan's Outline on a Re-narrate.
- **Narrated purchases:**
  - `extract` returns the item, its category, the payer (the person who pays, never an account or a card) and, when the prose says so, how they paid, in the prose's words;
  - the engine prices it in this order: a price the player set on a chip (*Set price*, *Edit amount*), charged as set; the catalog row that names the item (by id, or `matchCatalog`: whole words with simple plurals, the item's main noun among the row's head nouns and no qualifier of the item's making it another kind of the row, except that a qualifier no row of the noun names, "large" in "large coffee", lets the place choose: a row it kept out fits when the place sells the row's category and not that of any row that fits outright, so a large coffee is "Drip coffee, 12 oz, cafe" at a cafe and "Coffee, ground" at a grocery or where nothing sold tells them apart; then the row covering most of the item's words, the purchase's category, what the place sells); the stated price; the band of the purchase's category, or of a category the item names. A price the player states in a Buy is charged in full (and declines when it cannot be paid); a price the narration alone gives is capped at the economy pack's `unpricedCap` (minor units; by default `derivedUnpricedCap`, the highest `price_cents` band maximum across the world's `economy.catalog` and `economy.categories`, else 500 major units; worlds saved with the old `unpricedCapCents` are migrated once). Build edits it in the economy pack's entry, a **Narrated-price cap** field (the economy ui's `config` fields) that shows the derived default and the world's currency; a cap that isn't a positive whole number is the lint warning `economy.unpriced_cap`, and the engine then rounds a positive fraction and reads anything else as unset. The buy's `economy.txn` records the narrated amount and whether it was capped or clamped, and every purchase chip shows the amount charged with "(priced as …)" and "narrated X, capped at Y" or "clamped to Y";
  - one purchase per item: the extract prompt asks for two things bought as two purchases, and a narrated purchase naming several things one of which is the turn's own Buy (`splitAlongBuy`, which holds a part to be the Buy as `boughtAlong` does, below) drops the Buy's part and prices the rest on its own, without the bundle's price ("fish and chips", which holds no part of the Buy, stays whole);
  - a narrated purchase of the turn's own Buy (`boughtAlong`) is dropped only when it is the Buy told again: the same main noun; one name's words holding the other's, where the Buy's words are its charged name's and the item the player typed for it ("large black coffee" after a typed "large coffee" charged as "Drip coffee, 12 oz, cafe"), or the catalog row `economy.buy` prices the purchase as where the player character is being the Buy's own; any stated price the Buy's (±25% of the charge or the typed price, or inside its row's band); and no "another" beside it in the prose. "Another", "a second", "one more", "for <person>", a different quantity or a qualifier that rules the Buy's out, in either of its names ("green" for "red", "small" for a typed "large") make it a new purchase; anything else of the same noun is unsure and always asks with an *Also bought …?* confirm chip, whatever the purchase mode. So does another noun whose catalog row (as `economy.buy` prices it where the player character is) is a sibling of a Buy's row, in the same group of the price table (Drip coffee and Latte are both Coffee), unless "another", "for <person>" or a different quantity makes it a new purchase: the reply's "coffee" after a typed latte may be the latte in other words, so it asks rather than charging a second drink. A chip's quote is its own purchase's, drawn afresh inside the row's band, so it needn't be what the Buy charged;
  - a narrated purchase nothing prices gets a decline chip, *Bought X: no price*, with *Set price* (the shared money input), *Pick item* (catalog rows close to it, then what the place sells, from `economy.item_choices`) and *Dismiss*; an automatic one keeps *Remove it* and *Accept* beside them;
  - a Buy may say how it is paid, and the extract step passes the prose's words the same way: "credit card" pays from a card, "debit card" or "checking" from checking, "cash" from the wallet, "savings" from savings. A kind of account the payer doesn't have falls back: a card to checking, anything else to the usual order. When the extractor names an account as the payer, its holder pays from that account; when it names a thing someone owns (a debit card in the wallet), its owner pays the way the name says (a debit card from checking, a credit card on the card, a wallet in cash). A method the proposal states wins over all of these;
  - for a typed Buy, when `interpret` leaves the way of paying out, the player's own words fill it ("credit card", "debit card", "cash" or "savings"), but only when said as a payment: after "with", "on", "using", "by", "from" or "in" ("pay with my credit card", "in cash", "from savings"), and not as a thing named after it ("a credit card holder"). A negated mention ("not with cash", "instead of …", "rather than …") is skipped and the last mention counts, so "not with cash, with my debit card" pays by debit card, "I buy a credit card holder for $12" doesn't charge the card, and a long sentence that says "pay with my credit card" charges the card, not checking. A method `interpret` set stands;
  - money the model states as written (8 for $8) is converted to minor units once, before the channels read it, in `extract` and in a world's own `effects` steps (their prompts ask for plain amounts in the world's currency), and a narrated transfer's chip shows the formatted amount;
  - a payment's named source pays only when it is the payer's own cash, checking, savings or card account, at every money level; a loan or a brokerage account named as the source falls back to the usual choice. A narrated transfer's sum is one rounded number in minor units (its cents when given, else the converted amount), and its chip states that sum;
  - a narrated give of money becomes a transfer of that amount with method "cash", converted once before dedupe. A give is money only when the whole item is: an optional article, an amount with the currency's symbol or code or a money word, or a cash word, then optionally "bill", "note" or "in cash" ("$20", "a twenty", "a $20 bill", "20 dollars in cash"). A priced thing ("my $300 jacket", "a $20 gift card") stays a thing. A Gear give never makes money into a thing: "cash", "$20", "a twenty" and "20 dollars" are refused as items ("2 old daggers" in pence is an item, since a letter currency symbol counts only beside the number);
  - each side of a narrated transfer is a person or a person's own cash, checking, savings or card account. An account stands for its holder, paying from it (`from`) or depositing into it (`to`), and two sides of one holder move money between two of their accounts, so money is conserved. A thing, a loan or brokerage account, or an account no person holds is refused with a *Didn't apply* chip, and nothing moves. The extract prompt states the contract: transfers are between people (entity ids) or their own cash, checking, savings or card accounts, never things, with method "cash" when bills or coins change hands in person ("hands me $20", "slips her a twenty") and no method for a bank transfer, an app payment or a card;
  - cash handed over (a transfer with method "cash") lands in the payee's carried cash, else checking, and comes from the payer's carried cash when it covers, else the usual source, so "Danny hands me $20 for gas" adds $20 to the wallet. A transfer's other stated method picks the payer's account the way a purchase's does, so *Pay another way* on a declined transfer tries each account;
  - a narrated transfer that tells again a movement of money the turn's own actions made (`movedAlong`): a withdrawal, a deposit, a move between accounts or a payment, as a posting among the turn's pre-reply events. It is dropped when both sides name that posting's and its amount is the purchase rule's near: within ±25% of what left the one side or reached the other (a machine's fee makes them differ), or no amount stated. A side names a posting's side as an account it drew on (`from`) or paid into (`to`), a party holding one, or a party whose outside account it moved. "I take $60 out of the machine" told as checking paying the wallet $60 moves the money once; a transfer of another amount, or to another account, keeps its chip;
  - in **lenient** worlds a narrated price inside the band is accepted, and one outside it is clamped with a chip. A capped or clamped purchase's chip has *Edit amount*: on a confirm chip it applies the purchase at the amount typed; on an automatic one's Undo chip `economy.reprice` posts the difference between the same two accounts.
  - **A decline after the prose** shows a chip: *Pay another way* (retries savings, then card, then checking, then cash), *Remove it* or *Accept*. By default the item stays unowned and the next Outcome slot says so ("the payment was declined; they don't have it"). Remove and Accept only reword that note. Notes go to the next turn and its swipes, and are dropped once a later message has committed and another follows. They are kept in `userStorage/runs/<chat>/outcome-notes.json`, so a restart before the next turn still tells it.

### 9.6 Scale and storage
- **Record targets and overflow:** §4.3.
- **Materialization** records only player-facing views and actions.
- **Previews** are never stored.
- **Turn-critical work** (simulation, catch-up and timelapse segments) runs in the backend process, inside the engine run, bounded by the deterministic unit caps (§9.2). A default-cap segment took 38 ms on a child process and 2.5 ms in process, far inside the host's 15 s watchdog, so a second process and a per-chat registry copy would have bought nothing (D9).
- **Dense routine worlds** stop a timelapse sooner: every block the POV could meet is lived at its minute (§8), so the unit cap's events run out before its agent-days (the dense record-size world, where everyone shares the POV's café, park and office, stops after 22 days, the needs the POV lives counted among its events), and the player presses Continue for the rest, which keeps a typed span's hold on the POV; caching `dayPlan` per state is the known way to win back the timing, and with it the room to give a segment more events and so more days.
- **One worker.** The backend runs one task at a time, so a request or a step that holds it holds everything else, a generation's guard and the engine run included. Every synchronous stretch stays well under a second. I measured on a large private world (257 places, 521 entities, an 844-message chat), with the host's reads simulated: the longest stretch was about 0.2–0.35 s, either one GM view or one read of the chat.
  - **Chat reads.** The host reads a chat only whole: every message, with all its reasoning (174 MB on that chat). The backend keeps a copy of each chat's messages (eight chats at most) and patches it from the host's message events (sent, edited, swiped, deleted) and from its own metadata writes. It reads the chat afresh:
    - after each generation that saved text, because the host saves the reply and its reasoning itself (the guard's stop saves none, so the engine run reads the copy);
    - when an event can't be applied in place;
    - when a chat's ledger starts over (`forget`).

    Once a chat is open, a drawer request reads none of its messages and a turn reads them once.
  - **Read passes.** A view, the GM view, the fact lines and the engine run's sections are each built in a read pass (`readPass`), after the folds, with synchronous reads only. Within a pass, each of these is gathered once (`passMemo`), keyed by the state and world it reads:
    - each travel mode's route graph;
    - the economy's flows, account holders, pass-through shares and contract work;
    - the hidden-name check.

    Applying an event to a state drops what was kept for it. Nothing is kept outside a pass, so a simulation that changes its states in place always reads them afresh.
  - **Pure memos.** Each of these is a pure function of its text, so it is kept across passes, with the oldest dropped first:
    - content ids, 4,096 kept;
    - name spellings and compiled name patterns, 8,192 kept;
    - compiled expressions, 4,096 kept.
  - **Pushes.** A change schedules a push 40 ms later. While one push builds, at most one more waits behind it, and it carries every change made in the meantime.
  - **Giving way.** Every ledger operation and every pushed view yields to the event loop before it folds (`giveWay`), so a message already waiting is answered first.
- **Background lane** (one child process, `dist/worker/background-lane.js`): Check world, Playtest, imports, Build with AI validation. It sends heartbeats within the host watchdog (15 s) and never blocks a turn.
- **Jobs** are kept per user in `userStorage/jobs.json`: kind, world, input, status, progress, message and result. A request for a kind the runner doesn't have, or with an input that isn't an object, is refused.
